Madonnas  are  all  of  the  same  general  type.  His  "Birth 
of  Venus,"  "Spring,"  and  "Mars  and  Venus,"  which  were 
all  painted  for  the  Medici  villa  at  Costello,  are  steeped 
in  the  sentiment  of  renascent  classicism — a  classicism 
which  was  as  yet  spontaneous  and  childlike;  while  his 
frescoes  in  Pope  Sixtus  IV.'s  chapel  are  scarcely  less 
interesting  in  another  way — because,  that  is,  they  are 
full  of  materials  drawn  from  classical  architecture  and 
sculpture.  It  is  evident  that,  like  another  pupil  (and, 
indeed,  the  reputed  son)  of  Fra  Lippo,  Filippino  Lippi 
(1457-1507),  who  filled  many  sketch-books  with  drawings 
of  designs  and  details  taken  from  them,  Botticelli  was 
an  ardent  student  of  the  monuments  of  ancient  Rome, 
the  power  and  beauty  of  which  men  were  just  then 
beginning  to  appreciate.  It  is  the  more  curious,  there- 
fore, that  late  in  life  Botticelli  underwent  an  intellectual 
reaction,  devoted  himself  to  the  illustration  of  Dante, 
and,  finally,  becoming  a  disciple  of  Savonarola,  aban- 

►  doned  art  entirely.     The  power  of  the  classic  revival, 


Mantcgna  and  Signorelli  189 

together  with  nearly  all  the  new  forces  which  were 
affecting  art  at  the  time,  is  even  more  conspicuously 
shown  in  Andrea  Mantegna  (1431-1506),  perhaps  the  ' 
greatest  figure  in  Italian  art,  outside  Venice,  during  the 
second  half  of  the  fifteenth  century.  He  was  a  personal 
friend  of  some  of  the  leading  humanists;  as  a  youth  he 
studied  with  avidity  the  collection  of  antiquities  made 
by  his  master  Squarcione ;  later  on,  he  spent  his  wealth 
freely  in  making  such  a  collection  of  his  own ;  and 
though  his  paintings  deal  generally  with  the  conven- 
tional religious  themes — his  "Triumph  of  Julius  Caesar" 
at  Hampton  Court  is  the  most  important  exception — 
they  are  everywhere  imbued  with  the  severe  spirit  of 
ancient  art.  With  Mantegna  we  may  join  Luca  Signorelli  - 
(1441-1523),  in  whom  the  passion  for  nature  combined  * 
with  the  passion  for  antiquity.  Though  he  has  been 
called  "the  Dante  of  fifteenth  century  painting" — a  title  ' 
which  is  not  inappropriate  in  view  of  the  intensity  of 
his  work — he  treated  themes  from  the  "Purgatorio" 
and  from  Ovid's  "  Metamorphoses'*  in  precisely  the 
same  way,  and  in  his  religious  frescoes  in  the  Cathedral 
at  Orvieto  no  less  than  in  his  "Education  of  Pan,"  he 
made  the  nude  figure  for  the  first  time  a  prominent 
element  in  the  composition.  Symonds  has  said  of  him 
that  he  thus  "powerfully  promoted  the  rehabilitation 
of  the  body  achieved  for  art  by  the  Renaissance."  * 

A  marked  general  feature  in  the  evolution  of  Italian 
painting  at  this  time  is  suggested  here,  which  is  so 
important  that  we  may  fittingly  pause  to  emphasise  it. 
Still  very  largely  under  the  direct  control  of  the  Church, 
painting  remained  for  the  most  part  nominally  re- 
Jigious;  that  is,  the  traditional  themes  from  Bible  story 
and  the  lives  of  the  saints  continued  to  provide  the 
principal  matter  for  the  artist's  skill.  Yet  it  needs 
*  "The  Fine  Arts,"  p.  213. 


190  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

only  the  most  superficial  acquaintance  with  its  history 
to  perceive  that  meanwhile  art  was  being  gradually 
transformed  from  within.  The  old  subjects  were  re- 
produced, but  they  were  handled  now  in  a  spirit  in- 
creasingly different  from  that  which  had  characterised 
such  men  as  Giotto  and  Fra  Angelico.  We  have  only 
to  turn  from  either  of  these  thoroughly  devout  and 
single-hearted  workmen  who,  however  imperfect  their 
technique,  at  least  put  their  souls  and  their  faith  into 
what  they  painted,  to  such  an  artist,  say,  as  Ghir- 
landajo,  to  realise  that  genuine  religious  feeling  has 
vanished  or  is  fast  vanishing,  and  that  the  thought  of 
the  artist  is  now  concerned  primarily,  not  with  the 
doctrinal  bearings  of  his  subject,  but  with  its  pictorial 
effects.  These  tendencies,  of  course,  reflected  the 
changing  spirit  of  an  age  of  growing  humanism, 
inquiry,  and  general  indifference  to  spiritual  things;  for 
a  time  they  were  apparent  in  certain  painters  only;  but 
we  shall  witness  their  further  developments  in  the  art 
of  the  following  century.  Here,  however,  it  should 
be  remembered  that  this  change  in  the  direction  of  the 
secularisation  of  art  even  in  the  treatment  of  sacred 
topics  had  another  side.  It  has  been  pointed  out  that 
Fra  Lippo  Lippi,  bound  down  as  he  was  to  such  sacred 
topics,  was  apt  to  take  unwarrantable  liberties  with 
them,  making  his  angels  out  of  street  urchins  and 
painting  the  portraits  of  his  peasant  loves  for  his 
virgins  and  saints;  and  he  has  been  severely  blamed 
for  doing  this.  But  it  must  be  recognised  that  this  was 
his  way — and  the  only  way  that  was  open  to  him — 
of  escape  from  the  narrowly  conventional  restrictions  of 
religious  painting  into  that  wider  naturalism  of  which 
his  generation  was  in  quest.  A  parallel  may  be  in- 
stituted here  between  these  early  naturalists  in  art  and 
the  last  of  the  three  Greek  tragic  poets.     By  the  rules 


Secularisation  of  Painting  191 

of  the  Attic  stage,  Euripides  was  forced  against  the  true 
bias  of  his  genius  and  sympathies  to  treat  those  colossal 
themes  of  the  heroic  past  out  of  which  his  predecessors 
had  made  their  tragedies.  But  the  spirit  of  reverence 
and  piety  had  by  this  time  gone,  and  in  his  hands  the 
ancient  legends  become  realistic  melodramas,  and  the 
gigantic  figures  of  old,  Athenian  men  and  women  of  his 
own  century.  While  preserving  the  traditional  frame- 
work of  Attic  tragedy,  Euripides  thus  gave  it  an  entirely 
new  tone  and  meaning,  bringing  that  which  had  been 
remote  and  superhuman  down  to  the  plane  of  actual 
life.  In  much  the  same  way  the  Italian  painters  of 
the  early  Renaissance,  dealing  with  subjects  which  were 
remote  and  superhuman,  brought  their  art  down  to  the 
plane  of  actual  life,  maintaining  its  forms  as  prescribed 
by  Church  and  convention,  but  radically  modifying  its 
character.  This  is  the  general  significance  of  the  plea 
for  a  freer  realistic  art,  which  Browning  has  put  into 
the  mouth  of  his  Fra  Lippo  Lippi : 

The  beauty  and  the  wonder  and  the  power, 

The  shapes  of  things,  their  colours,  lights  and  shades, 

Changes,  surprises — and  God  made  it  all ! 

For  what  ?     Do  you  feel  thankful,  ay  or  no, 

For  this  fair  town's  face,  yonder  river's  line, 

The  mountain  round  it  and  the  sky  above, 

Much  more  the  figures  of  man,  woman,  child, 

These  are  the  frame  to  ?     What's  it  all  about  ? 

To  be  passed  over,  despised  ?  or  dwelt  upon, 

Wondered  at  ?     Oh,  this  last,  of  course !  you  say. 

But  why  not  do  as  well  as  say — paint  these 

Just  as  they  are,  careless  what  comes  of  it? 

God's  works — paint  any  one,  and  count  it  crime 

To  let  a  truth  slip. 

Men  woke  up  to  find  that  there  were  more  things  in 
heaven  and  earth  worth  painting  than  had  been  dreamed 
of  in  the  philosophy  of  the  schools.  But  the  time  was 
not  yet  ripe  for  a  frankly  realistic  art.     Painting  was 


192  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

still  in  bondage  to  the  Church.  They  had  therefore  to 
incorporate  their  newly  awakened  interest  in  the  things 
and  people  about  them  with  the  materials  of  religious 
doctrine  and  sacred  story  upon  which  they  were  com- 
pelled to  work. 

While  we  have  not  here  to  concern  ourselves  with 
the  development  of  technique  as  such,  it  is  necessary, 
none  the  less,  to  note  that  this  was  an  important  factor 
in  widening  the  scope  of  art  during  the  fifteenth  century, 
and  particularly  in  the  growth  of  naturalism.  Science 
now  came  to  the  help  of  genius.  In  considering  the 
limitations  of  a  painter  like  Giotto  we  must  never  forget 
how  much  he  was  hampered  by  imperfect  knowledge 
and  the  meagreness  of  his  resources.  The  painters  of 
the  next  age,  however  they  might  compare  with  him 
in  natural  power,  had  at  least  a  greater  capacity  of  ex- 
pression ;  they  were  able  to  do  so  much  more  than  he 
had  done,  in  part  because  they  knew  more  and  had  more 
adequate  resources  at  their  command.  The  direct  study 
of  the  living  model,  and,  as  in  the  case  of  men  like 
Antonio  Pollaiuolo  (1433-98)  and  Signorelli,  of  the  dead 
body,  was  a  matter  of  incalculable  moment  in  the  educa- 
tion of  the  artist  for  the  treatment  of  his  human  figures ; 
until  mastery  of  the  elements  of  anatomy  had  become  a 
regular  stage  in  his  preparation  for  his  work,  progress 
in  this  direction  was  manifestly  impossible.  The  appli- 
cation of  exact  science  to  painting  was  another  potent 
influence  in  artistic  development;  as,  for  example,  in 
the  matter  of  perspective.  In  this,  the  great  Florentine 
bronze  worker,  Lorenzo  Ghiberti  (1378-1455)  had 
pointed  the  way.  But  the  painter^svlio  first  resolutely  set 
himself  to  master  its  problems  was  that  Paolo  di  Dono 
who  has  already  been  mentioned  under  his  familiar 
name  of  Uccello — a  name  which  he  is  supposed  to  have 
owed  to  his  special  fondness  for  painting  birds.     The 


Rise  of  Oil  Painting  193 

ardour  with  which  he  pursued  his  studies  in  this  new 
branch  of  technical  scholarship  bordered  on  fanaticism ; 
he  sacrificed  his  financial  welfare  and  his  domestic  peace 
to  them ;  it  is  said  that  he  would  sit  up  over  them  the 
whole  night  long,  and  that  his  wife  could  wring  from 
him  no  other  answer  to  her  appeals  than  the  ever- 
repeated  :  "O,  che  dolce  cosa  e  questa  prospettiva  ! " — 
"O,  what  a  sweet  thing  is  this  perspective!"  But 
however  unwise  he  may  have  been  from  the  point  of 
view  of  his  personal  interests,  he  greatly  advanced  his 
art;  and  from  his  time  onward,  theory  going  hand  in 
hand  with  practice,  the  question  of  scientific  perspective 
received  increasingly  careful  attention. 

Without  entering  further  into  details,  we  can  now 
see  how  the  enlargement  of  the  scope  of  art,  and  its 
transformation  from  a  vehicle  of  religious  ideas  and 
sentiments  into  a  means  of  reproducing  and  interpreting 
the  most  varied  aspects  of  nature  and  human  life,  were 
aided  by  its  rapid  evolution  along  technical  lines.  One 
other  influence  which  helped  to  change  it  in  a  very  vital 
way  has  still  to  be  mentioned.  This  was  the  establish- 
ment of  the  modern  method  of  oil  painting.  Tradition 
has  attributed  the  invention  of  this  to  the  two  Flemish 
painters,  the  brothers  Van  Eyck  (Hubert,  i37o?-i426; 
Jan,  i3go?-i44o).  Their  title  to  such  distinction  and  the 
real  nature  of  their  innovation  are  now  matters  of 
dispute.  It  is  at  any  rate  certain  that  a  new  process 
of  preparing,  using,  or  drying  colours  was  introduced, 
about  the  middle  of  the  fifteenth  century,  and  gave  rise 
to  what  we  specifically  know  as  oil  painting;  and  what 
we  have  here  to  realise  is  the  way  in  which  this  affected 
the  general  destinies  of  art.  The  spread  of  oil  painting  \ 
greatly  strengthened  the  spirit  of  individualism  in  art,  '' 
for  it  developed  and  popularised  the  easel  picture  as  a  /' 
rival  to  the  mural  painting.    So  long  as  mural  painting 


ig4  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

provided,  not  indeed  the  only,  but  the  principal  occupa- 
tion of  the  artist,  his  efforts  were  mainly  confined  to 
the  decoration  of  ecclesiastical  and  public  buildings. 
He  was,  in  fact,  the  servant  of  the  Church  or  the  City, 
and  what  he  did  was  conditioned  and  defined  by  the 
character  of  his  service.  The  introduction  of  oil  paint- 
ing brought  with  it  a  marked  change  in  his  status.  It 
meant  that  he  no  longer  had  to  wait  for  his  commission 
and  to  undertake  only  what  was  assigned  to  him;  a 
portable  canvas  enabled  him  to  do  his  own  work  on  his 
own  impulse  and  in  his  own  studio.  It  meant,  too, 
that  he  had  a  larger  and  more  varied  public  to  work 
for,  and  this  reacted  upon  the  quality  of  his  production 
and  greatly  favoured  the  general  tendency  towards 
breadth,  eclecticism,  realism,  and  the  growth  of  the  lay 
spirit.  As  a  factor  of  importance  in  the  transformation 
of  Renaissance  art,  the  rise  of  oil  painting,  and  with 
it  of  the  private  purchaser,  therefore  deserves  emphasis. 
A  comparison  between  the  effects  of  this  and  those 
produced  by  the  printing  press  is  too  obvious  to  need 
elaboration. 


IV. — THE   GOLDEN   AGE   OF   ITALIAN   ART 

We  now  come  to  the  closing  years  of  the  fifteenth 
century,  and  cross  the  boundary  line  into  the  sixteenth. 
This  brings  us  to  the  golden  age  of  Italian  painting — 
the  age  of  Michelangelo  Buonarrotti(i475-i564),  Raffaelo 
Sanzio,  or  Raphael  (1483-1520),  and  Antonio  Allegri, 
called  Correggio  from  the  name  of  his  birthplace  (1494?- 
1534);  of  the  great  Venetians,  Giovanni  Bellini  (1428- 
15 16),  Giorgio  Barbarelli,  known  as  Giorgione  (1476- 
151 1),  Tiziano  Vicello,  or  Titian  (i4go?-i576),  Jacopo 
Robusti,  whose  familiar  name  of  Tintoretto,  or  the 
little  dyer,   was  derived  from  his  father's  occupation 


Renaissance  Architecture  195 

(1518-94),  Paolo  Caliari,  or  Veronese  (1528-88),  and 
of  many  another  master  only  less  famous  than  these. 
On  the  threshold  of  this  wonderful  epoch  stands  the 
mighty  figure  of  that  epitome  of  the  Renaissance  in  all 
its  many-sidedness,  Leonardo  da  Vinci  (1452-1519),  who 
belongs  to  it  in  spirit  though  chronologically  his  activity 
as  a  painter  falls  mainly  within  the  preceding  genera- 
tion. The  life  of  Michelangelo,  on  the  other  hand, 
carries  us  far  forward  into  that  age  of  silver  which 
followed  rapidly  upon  the  age  of  gold. 

Throughout  the  whole  range  of  Italian  art  during 
this  period  of  its  culmination,  the  influences  of 
humanism  and  of  the  universal  mania  for  the  antique 
are  everywhere  apparent.  It  is  in  architecture,  of 
course,  that  they  are  shown  in  the  most  formal  way. 
Already  that  style  which  we  call  "Renaissance"  had 
been  firmly  established  in  Italy,  whence  it  had  also 
begun  to  spread  to  the  countries  north  of  the  Alps ;  and 
as  the  origin  of  such  style  is  to  be  sought  in  the  revival 
of  classic  forms,  it  is  significant  that  it  should  have 
taken  its  rise,  not  as  the  literary  movement  had  done, 
in  Florence,  but  in  Rome.  It  is  true  that  the  great 
leader  of  the  new  taste,  Filippo  Brunelleschi  (1377-1466), 
was  a  Florentine,  and  that  his  most  important  work 
was  done  in  his  native  city;  but  it  was  during  his  early 
residence  in  Rome  that  classical  enthusiasm  was  first 
awakened  within  him.  It  is  a  singular  fact  that  down 
to  this  time  it  had  never  occurred  to  Roman  architects 
to  imitate  their  ancient  buildings;  careless  of  the  irre- 
parable damage  done,  they  quarried  in  them  freely  for 
materials  for  their  own  uses,  but  did  not  dream  of  going 
to  them  for  inspiration  or  instruction.  The  growing 
spirit  of  humanism  now  led  to  the  rediscovery  of  the 
art  no  less  than  of  the  literature  of  classical  antiquity. 
The  search   for  manuscripts  was  accompanied  by  an 


196  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

equally  zealous  search  for  ancient  statues  and  fragments 
of  decorative  sculpture,  ornaments,  medals  and  gems, 
and  with  such  recovered  treasures  nobles  and  cardinals 
now  filled  their  palaces.  The  systematic  study  of 
classical  architecture  was  one  phase  of  this  general  re- 
birth of  the  imperial  past.  The  value  of  the  Roman 
monuments  was  now  fully  realised.  Raphael  himself 
was  for  a  time  inspector  of  them;  and,  addressing  the 
Pope  in  a  famous  memorandum  of  1518  or  1519,  he 
bitterly  complains  of  the  destruction  which  was  still 
going  on  among  them,  imploring  his  Holiness  to  take 
under  his  own  protection  whatever  remained  to  testify 
to  the  greatness  and  power  of  the  divine  spirit  of  anti- 
quity. As  a  matter  of  personal  interest,  it  may  be 
added  that  the  illustrious  painter's  premature  death  was 
caused  by  a  fever  contracted  while  he  was  superintend- 
ing some  excavations  among  the  city's  ruins. 

Such,  then,  was  the  impulse  behind  Renaissance 
architecture,  which,  entering  though  it  now  did  upon 
a  period  of  almost  unbroken  supremacy  all  over  Europe, 
yet  found  its  most  magnificent  example  in  St.  Peter's  at 
Rome.  This  mighty  witness  to  human  ambition  and 
power — a  fitting  embodiment  of  the  spirit  of  the  worldly 
Julius  and  of  the  genius  of  its  chief  architect,  Michel- 
angelo— was  "designed  to  be  the  eternal  monument  of 
the  glory  and  universality  of  Catholicism,"  while  it  has 
in  fact  become  "the  most  impressive  memorial  of  its 
decay."*  Wandering  down  its  vast  aisles,  amid  splen- 
dours of  art  which  only  too  eloquently  proclaim  the 
pagan  temper  of  its  builders,  the  thoughtful  student 
can  hardly  fail  to  be  reminded  afresh  of  the  real  mean- 
ing of  the  great  contemporary  Teutonic  revolt  against 
the  debased  religion  for  which  it  stood — a  revolt,  he 
will  further  remember,  precipitated  by  the  means  em- 
*  Lecky,  "  History  of  Rationalism,"  chap.  iii. 


Renaissance  Sculpture  197 

ployed  in  the  collection  of  money  for  its  completion. 
He  will  see  in  it,  moreover,  in  another  way,  a  colossal 
symbol  of  the  new  era  which  had  now  dawned  upon 
the  world.  Gothic  architecture  had  been  essentially  • 
Christian  architecture — the  expression  of  the  feelings 
and  aspirations  of  the  centuries  during  which  faith  had , 
been  genuine  and  devotion  intense.  In  the  substitution  , 
of  Renaissance  architecture,  which,  though  pagan  in 
source  and  inspiration,  was  none  the  less  applied  to 
the  purposes  even  of  Christian  worship,  we  have  a  strik- 
ing illustration  of  the  immense  and  fundamental  changes 
which  were  at  work  throughout  the  entire  range  of  man's 
moral  and  intellectual  life.  The  historian  whom  I 
have  just  quoted  notes  the  significance  of  the 
fact  that  "Cologne  Cathedral,  the  last  of  the  great 
mediaeval  .works,  remained  unfinished,  while  the 
whole  energies  of  Europe  were  concentrated  upon  the 
church  of  St .  Peter  at  Rome ."  In  this  fact  we 
have  an  index,  not  only  of  the  world-wide  variation 
in  taste,  but  also  of  a  general  collapse  of  the  ancient 
fabric  of  thought. 

Sculpture  tells  us  much  the  same  tale.  We  have 
seen  that  the  new  movement  was  anticipated  by  Niccol& 
Pisano's  imitations  of  bas-reliefs  on  Roman  sarcophagi. 
Then  Lorenzo  Ghiberti,  who  wrought  the  wonderful 
scriptural  designs  on  the  two  great  bronze  doors  of  the 
Baptistery  at  Florence,  broke  through  the  narrowing 
conventions  of  existing  plastic  art  by  a  direct  appeal  to 
nature.  "From  my  earliest  days,"  he  declared,  "I  have 
always  tried  to  discover  how  nature  reveals  herself  in 
art,  and  how  I  may  best  draw  near  her;  how  forms  really 
present  themselves  to  the  eye,  and  on  what  principles 
the  arts  of  painting  and  sculpture  should  be  practised." 
Another  master  of  naturalism  appeared  at  the  same  time 
in  the  greatest  of  the  early  Tuscan  sculptors,  Donati  di 


198  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

Betto  Bardi,  or  Donatello_(i386-i466),  in  whom,  how- 
ever, we  also  mark  "the  glowing  strength  of  classic  in- 
spiration. A  student  in  Rome,  along  with  his  friend 
Brunelleschi,  of  ancient  architecture  and  sculpture,  he 
there  caught  the  finest  spirit  of  the  antique,  while  he  yet 
remained  absolutely  true  to  his  own  individual  quality 
of  genius  and  to  the  living  types  of  manhood  and 
womanhood  which  he  set  himself  to  portray.  While  his 
free  use  of  the  nude,  even  in  figures  of  angels  and  in 
his  David,  is  thus  an  important  characteristic  of  his 
work,  the  absence  of  anything  suggesting  lifeless  imita- 
tion of  ancient  models  is  even  more  important.  In  him, 
in  fact,  we  perceive  the  full  advantages  of  the  classical 
revival  in  its  early  stages,  and  before,  as  Symonds  puts 
it,  "pseudo-classicism  and  pedantry  had  .  .  .  distorted 
the  judgment"  and  "misdirected  the  aim  of  artists,"  and 
while  as  yet  "contact  with  the  antique  world  served  only 
to  stimulate  original  endeavour."*  But  the  later  growth 
of  an  undiscriminating  enthusiasm  for  everything 
antique  led  sculptors  to  ignore  altogether  the  essential 
difference  between  classic  and  Christian  sentiment,  and 
thus  to  deprive  their  work  of  all  the  devotional  signifi- 
cance which  should  attach  to  religious  themes.  The 
lovely  glazed  polychrome  bas-reliefs  of  Luca  della 
Robbia  (1399-1482)  and  his  school  are  full  of  the 
tenderest  spiritual  emotion,  and  there  were  other  men 
of  the  time  who  carried  forward  the  traditions  of  a 
thoroughly  earnest  Christian  art.  But  the  currents  of 
thought  and  feeling  were  now  running  strongly  in  a 
different  direction,  and  sculpture,  while  more  and  more 
concerning  itself  (as  in  the  case  of  Benvenuto  Cellini) 
with  pagan  mythology,  exhibited  its  change  of  temper 
even  more  suggestively  in  the  entirely  detached  and  in- 
different manner  in  which  it  handled  Christian  motives.

Renaissance  Sculpture  199 

Before  the  fifteenth  century  was  out  the  secularisation  ' 
of  sculpture  was  practically  complete.  "For  an  artist 
at  this  time,"  says  Symonds,  "a  Greek  myth  and  a 
Christian  legend  were  all  one  "  ;*  whichever  he  selected, 
his  main  interest  was  in  its  artistic  possibilities,  and  his 
chief  business  to  make  it  the  occasion  for  the  display  of 
his  knowledge  of  anatomy  and  his  technical  skill.  An 
illustration  is  at  hand  in  the  work  of  Jacopo  Tatti,  or 
Sansovino  (1486-1570),  whom  we  may,  with  Symonds, 
regard  as  marking  "the  final  intrusion  of  paganism  into 
modern  art."  Sculpture,  which  had  long  been  used,  in 
subordination  to  architecture,  for  the  devotional  adorn- 
ment of  churches,  had  now  liberated  itself  from  its 
servitude,  and  as  an  independent  art  was  addressing  . 
itself  frankly  to  the  further  stimulation  of  that  sense  of 
the  beauty  and  value  of  earthly  things  which  had  grown  * 
up  with  the  classic  revival.  If  to  the  artist  of  that  time 
even  a  fragment  of  antique  sculpture,  and  still  more 
the  Apollo  Belvedere,  the  Laocoon,  the  Venus  of  the 
Vaticanf  had  come  as  a  "revelation  of  antique  civilisa- 
tion, of  the  palestra  and  the  stadium,  of  the  sanctifica- 
tion  of  the  body,  of  the  apotheosis  of  man,  of  the 
religion  of  life,  and  nature,  and  joy,"J  the  labours  of 
the  modern  sculptor  who  took  such  ancient  productions 
as  his  models,  were  similarly  charged  with  a  message  of 
freedom  and  strength  and  physical  beauty.  Despite  > 
his  own  Dantean  seriousness,  the  intensity  of  his  reli- 
gious convictions,  and  that  vehement  quality  of  genius 
which  made  the  repose  and  temperance  of  Hellenism 
impossible  to  him,  it  was  to  this  pagan  school  of  art 
that  as  a  sculptor  even  Michelangelo  belonged.  His  , 
taste   was   shaped   among   the   antiquities  with   which 

*  "The  Fine  Arts,"   p.   123.

Lorenzo  de'  Medici  had  enriched  the  gardens  of  San 
Marco,  and  he  himself  asserted  that  the  Apollo  Belvedere 
had  been  his  true  master.  Hence,  as  we  may  see  in  his 
colossal  David,  who  is  simply  a  splendid  type  of  Greek 
athlete,  in  his  formidable  Moses,  who  has  been  not 
inaptly  described  as  "half  prize-fighter,  half  Jupiter 
Tonans,"  in  his  brooding  Jeremiah,  who  is  manifestly 
a  Greek  philosopher  in  meditation,  his  conspicuous 
failure  to  give  to  his  scriptural  subjects  the  appropriate 
tone  and  expression. 

It  will,  of  course,  be  evident  that  the  power  of 
classicism  could  not  show  itself  in  the  same  formal  and 
specific  way  in  painting  as  in  architecture  and  sculpture, 
for  while  in  these  fields  the  artist  was  directly  guided 
»  by  antique  models,  the  painter  felt  their  influence  for 
r  the  most  part  only  indirectly.  Yet  the  strength  of  that 
influence,  and  even  its  immediacy,  are  attested  in  many 
particulars.  We  note  it  most  obviously  in  the  immense 
•  popularity  of  subjects  taken  from  mythology,  which 
naturally  provided  a  multitude  of  motives  as  congenial 
to  the  painter's  brush  as  they  were  to  the  poet's  pen. 
We  note  it  in  the  spell  which  classical  art  and  literature 
clearly  exercised  over  the  memory  and  imagination  of 
the  artist,  even  when  he  was  working  upon  a  totally 
different  range  of  themes.  Thus,  to  take  a  single  ex- 
ample, when  Raphael,  in  his  famous  Vatican  picture, 
"The  Fire  in  the  Borgo,"  commemorates  the  miracle  by 
which  Leo  IV.  had  extinguished  a  conflagration  near 
St.  Peter's  by  the  sign  of  the  cross,  we  can  see  at  a 
glance  that  the  scene  he  is  really  depicting  is  the  destruc- 
tion of  Troy,  as  described  in  the  second  "-^neid,"  and 
that  the  group  in  the  left-hand  corner  is  in  fact  ^Eneas, 
with  Anchises  on  his  shoulders,  and  the  boy  Ascanius 
at  his  side,  in  flight  from  the  blazing  city.  As  a  more 
general  illustration  of  the  same  assimilative  and  accom- 


Decline  of  Religious  Art  201 

modating  tendency,  I  may  mention  the  increasing  popu- 
larity of  the  chubby  little  children  who  in  religious  / 
paintings  figure  as  cherubs,  but  in  whom  we  cannot, 
fail  to  recognise  under  very  slight  modification  the 
Cupids  of  ancient  art.  When  such  tendency  is  every-  • 
where  in  the  ascendant,  it  is  manifest  that  we  are  reach- 
ing the  end  of  specifically  religious  art. 

This  does  not  mean  that  the  spirit  of  devotion  is  as 
yet  extinct  in  Italian  painting.  In  the  earliest  and 
freshest  work  of  Perugino  (Pietro  Vannucci,  1466-1524),  , 
for  example,  we  have  perhaps  the  perfection  of  purely  • 
pietistic  art;  though  it  is  surely  a  significant  fact  that 
when  in  later  life  his  sordid  love  of  gain  grew  upon  him, 
and  (it  is  alleged)  he  even  became  an  unbeliever,  he 
continued  to  drive  a  thriving  trade  by  the  industrious 
reproduction  of  Virgins,  saints,  and  Holy  Families,  all 
alike  steeped  in  the  spirit  of  vapid  emotionalism.  In 
the  work  of  Francesco  Raibolino,  or  Francia  (1450- 
1517),  again,  we  have  genuine  religious  feeling.  Two 
painters  of  whom  mention  has  already  been  made 
in  a  previous  chapter  in  connection  with  Savonarola's 
preaching  also  deserve  a  place  in  this  survey  for 
their  success  in  preserving  the  quality  of  a  truly 
Christian  art.  One  of  these  is  Lorenzo  di  Credi 
(1459-1537),  who,  notwithstanding  his  strong  interest 
in  the  technical  side  of  his  craft,  remained  practi- 
cally untouched  by  the  paganising  movement  of  his 
time,  though  he  occasionally  yielded  to  the  blandish- 
ments of  Peruginesque  sentimentality.  The  other  is 
Fra  Bartolommeo,  also  known  as  Baccio  della  Porta  < 
(1476-15 17),  who,  having  as  we  have  seen  burned  his 
nude  studies  in  the  bonfire  of  vanities,  became  a 
Dominican,  and,  later  returning  to  painting,  devoted 
himself  henceforth  entirely  to  religious  work.  Yet,  on 
the  whole,  every  influence  of  this  new  age  made  towards  . 


i>2  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

what  I  have  called  the  secularisation  of  pictorial  art. 
In  its  increasing  range  of  subjects  painting  rejected  the 
control  of  theology,  and  asserted  its  right  to  go  out 
into  the  world  and  back  over  history  and  legend  for 
matter  for  pictorial  treatment;  and  in  the  spirit  of  its 
workmanship,  whether  the  themes  selected  were  sacred 
or  profane,  it  announced  that  henceforth  its  primary 
concern  was  with  life  and  character,  and  with  the 
technical  problems  of  expression  and  grouping, 
anatomy,  drawing,  perspective,  light,  shade,  colour. 

This  is  the  central  feature  of  the  transformation  of  art 
in  the  season  of  the  full  Renaissance;  and  whatever 
religious  fervour  may  still  be  detected  in  the  work  of 
some  of  the  greatest  artists — of  such  men,  for  instance, 
as  Michelangelo  and  Raphael — this  must  not  be  allowed 
to  obscure  for  us  the  reality  and  significance  of  the 
change.  The  painter  was  still  willing  enough  to  yield 
his  service  to  a  Church  which  on  its  side  was  equally 
willing  to  accept  and  applaud  the  skill  and  dexterity  of 
his  renderings  of  sacred  things,  without  inquiring  too 
curiously  into  their  Christian  value.  But  his  art  was 
no  longer  the  submissive  handmaid  of  theology.  It 
was,  like  sculpture,  independent;  like  sculpture,  it  re- 
.  joiced  in  strength  and  beauty,  freedom  and  greatness ; 
like  sculpture,  it  helped  in  the  emancipation  of  thought 
from  the  cramping  asceticism  of  mediaeval  ethics.  The 
development  of  painting  thus  falls  into  its  place  in  our 
story  as  one  phase  of  the  Renaissance  discovery  of  the 
world  and  man. 

The  evolution  of  painting  as  interpretation  might  be 
illustrated  by  the  successive  changes  which  marked  the 
treatment  of  any  of  the  standard  motives  of  Christian 
art.  The  Madonna  will  serve  as  an  example.  We  need 
not  go  farther  back  than  the  times  of  Fra  Angelico  and 
Giotto  to  find  this  subject  handled  under  the  strictest 


The  Madonna  in  Art  203 

conditions  and  with  all  the  accessories  of  purely  religious 
art.     The  aim  of  the  artist  is  not  to  portray  but  to  ex- 
press— not  to  represent  a  woman  and  a  mother,  or  even 
abstract  womanhood  and  motherhood,  but  to  embody c* 
certain  ideas  and  feelings,  and  thereby  to  awaken  the"* 
reverence  and  piety  of  the  spectator.     The  figure  of  the 
Virgin  is  therefore  conceived  in  an  entirely  conventional 
way,  and  is  so  designed  as  to  bring  out  all  the  dignity 
which  should  belong  to  the  Mother  of  God  and  the 
Queen  of  Heaven ;  every  trace  of  realism  is  rigorously 
excluded  as  destructive  of  such  dignity;  while,  on  the 
other  hand,  symbolism  is  freely  used  to  enforce  doctrinal 
truth  and  to  heighten  the  devotional  effect.     Hence  the 
remoteness  of  these  early   Madonnas  who,    "with   no 
limbs  beneath  their  robes,"*  and  no  hint  of  common 
human  emotion  on  their  smooth,  bland  faces,  belong 
wholly  to  the  range  of  hieratic  things  and  have  no  point 
of  connection  with  nature  and  ordinary  life.     But  by 
little  and  little  the  human  elements  encroach ;  domestic 
detail  begins  to  mingle  with  and  to  displace  the  sym- 
bolism ;  and  the  Mother  of  God  and  Queen  of  Heaven, 
while  as  yet  by  no  means  deidealised,  becomes  in  the 
hands  of  painters  like  Fra  Lippo  Lippi,   Bellini,  and 
Botticelli  more  and  more  of  a  woman.     In  Raphael  we 
note  a  conflict  of  tendencies;  occasionally,   as  in  the 
"Sistine  Madonna,"  he  makes1  glorious  use  of  all  the 
divine  suggestions  of  his  theme ;  but  more  often,  as  in 
the  "Belle  Jardiniere  "  and  the  "Madonna  della  Sedia," 
he  simply  paints  the  most  beautiful  woman  he  can  find 
as  the  representative  of  motherhood,  without  indicating, 
either  by  symbolism  or  by  general  tone  and  expression, 
the  transcendental  significance  of  his  type.     Upon  the  ' 
realistic  movement  which  such  works  exemplify  there  ' 
was  henceforth  but  little  check.     In  the  further  evolution 
*  Vernon  Lee,  "  Euphorion,"  i.  195. 


204  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

of  art  the  divine  suggestions,  and  even  the  ideal  quali- 
ties disappear,  and  all  that  is  left  in  the  Madonnas  of 
Andrea  del  Sarto,  of  Correggio,  and  pre-eminently  in 
those  of  the  Venetian  school,  is  the  beauty  of  the  human 
mother  with  the  human  child.  We  have  not  here  to 
discuss  the  religious  bearing  of  this  transformation.  We 
have  only  to  lay  stress  upon  it  as  a  fact  and  an  index. 

The  same  general  lines  of  evolution  are  as  clearly 
marked  if  we  consider  more  broadly  the  course  of  Italian 
painting  through  its  golden  age  to  its  decline,  as  this 
is  exemplified  in  the  work  of  any  of  its  representative 
masters. 

A  stern,  silent,  solitary  man,  Michelangelo,  it  might 
seem,  was  too  self-centred  and  independent,  and  too 
much  removed  from  the  common  currents  of  his  time, 
to  be  regarded  as  the  exponent  of  anything  but  his  own 
individual  taste.  Yet  when  we  remember  how  strongly 
he  was  influenced  in  different  ways  by  the  antique 
marbles  of  the  Medici  collections,  by  the  productions  of 
the  vigorous  and  realistic  sculptor,  Jacopo  della  Quercia 
(1374-1438),  by  the  paintings  of  Signorelli  and  Leonardo 
da  Vinci,  by  the  Platonic  philosophy  of  the  great 
humanists  whom  he  met  when  Lorenzo  the  Magnificent 
took  him  into  his  house,  by  the  poetry  of  Dante,  whose 
lofty  and  austere  temper  naturally  touched  his  own, 
and  by  the  sermons  of  Savonarola,  which  left  a  per- 
manent impress  on  the  tone  of  his  thought,  we  can  see 
that  he  was  not  a  man  without  ancestry;  in  the  univer- 
sality of  his  powers,  and  in  the  very  violence  of  his 
genius  and  its  expression,  he  stands  out  as  the  supreme 
challenge  of  the  new  age  of  personality  and  freedom 
against  the  starved  and  stunted  ideals  of  the  immediate 
past ;  while  the  extraordinary  complexity  of  his  character 
is  not  without  significance  to  one  who  considers  how 
many  forces  had  been  let  loose  in  that  new  age  which 


Michelangelo  205 

no  one  man,  however  great,  could  hope  to  bring  into 
harmonious  co-operation.  Combining,  like  Milton,  a 
stainless  purity  of  life  with  the  most  passionate  feeling 
for  sensuous  beauty,  he  maintained  his  integrity  and* 
his  Hebraic  zeal  for  righteousness  amid  all  the  fast- 
spreading  corruption  of  the  papal  capital.  When  he 
speaks  of  the  meaning  of  religious  art  to  the  artist,  it 
is  in  words  which  might  have  dropped  from  the  lips 
of  Fra  Angelico  himself:  "In  order  to  represent  the 
adored  image  of  our  Lord,  it  is  not  enough  that  a  master 
should  be  great  and  able.  I  maintain  that  he  must  also 
be  a  man  of  good  morals  and  conduct,  if  possible  a 
saint,  in  order  that  the  Holy  Ghost  may  give  him  in- 
spiration." Yet  despite  the  humble  and  devout  spirit 
which  these  words  express,  no  man  did  more  than  he 
who  uttered  them  to  destroy  the  religious  meaning  of 
traditional  Christian  art.  Here  is  the  paradox  of  Michel^ 
angelo's  character  and  career;  he  was  a  man  of  pro- 
foundly religious  feeling,  and  yet  his  influence  in  paint- 
ing no  less  than  in  architecture  and  sculpture  made  for 
secularisation.  Impatient  of  limitations  and  control;' 
arrogant,  impulsive,  and  audacious;  a  lover  of  the 
human  body,  which  for  him  was  the  one  great  revelation 
of  the  divine;*  a  worshipper  of  physical  strength,  which 
he  deemed  the  highest  manifestation  of  its  beauty;  he 
rejoiced  to  overleap  the  bounds  of  conventional  re- 
straints ;  to  charge  himself  with  almost  impossible  tasks 
that  he  might  show  his  skill  in  triumphing  over  all  their 
difficulties;  and  even  in  handling  his  favourite  subject, 
the  nude,  to  play  tricks  with  anatomy  to  the  extent  of 
exaggerating  muscular  development  and  pushing  bold- 

*  See,   for  example,  one  of  his  sonnets  to  Vittoria  Colonna  (as 
translated  by  Symonds  in  "The  Fine  Arts,"  p.  299)  : — 

"  Nor  hath  God   deigned  to  show  Himself  elsewhere 
More  clearly  than  in  human  forms  sublime ; 
Which,  since  they  image  Him,  compel  my  love." 


206  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

ness  of  attitude  to  almost  monstrous  extremes.  It  is 
therefore  not  surprising  that,  vast  as  was  the  range  of 
his  powers,  he  was  yet  prevented  by  the  essential  quali- 
ties of  his  temperament  and  his  genius  from  making 
even  the  most  promising  subjects  the  media  of  Christian 
teaching.  The  colossal  fresco  of  the  Last  Judgment  in 
the  Sistine  Chapel  stands  in  respect  of  its  motive  in 
direct  line  of  development  from  the  Last  Judgment  in 
the  Campo  Santo  at  Pisa.  But  while  this  latter  was 
imbued  with  the  religious  sentiments  of  its  time,  it  is 
difficult  to  detect  in  Michelangelo's  work  the  play  of 
any  distinctively  Christian  feeling.  What  trace  of  such 
feeling,  we  may  well  ask,  can  we  anywhere  find  in  the 
gestures  and  faces  of  these  "exasperated  giants,  some 
victorious,  some  vanquished,  all  naked  and  muscular 
as  athletes?  "  in  this  "avenging  Christ  with  His  hercu- 
lean frame  ?  "  in  this  "terrified  Virgin  who  cowers  beside 
her  Son  ?  "*  In  such  a  daringly  original  rendering  of 
a  traditional  subject  the  last  link  connecting  art  with 
Christian  tradition  has  been  broken.  As  a  titanic 
,  achievement  this  "Dies  Irae  "  is  probably  the  greatest 
thing  of  its  kind  in  existence.  But  it  is  simply  a  great 
picture.  To  regard  it  as  a  Christian  interpretation  of 
a  Christian  theme  would  be  absurd. 

Michelangelo  neither  sprang  from  nor  did  he  found 
a  school.  His  independence  and  originality  kept  him 
isolated.  Raphael,  on  the  other  hand,  so  absorbed  and 
so  gave  out  influence  as  to  become  the  epitome  and  the 
synthesis  of  all  that  is  most  characteristic  in  the  art  of 
his  time.  The  darling  of  the  papacy,  he  is  pre-emi- 
nently the  painter  of  the  power  and  prestige  of  Rome  in 
that  great  hour  of  its  strength  and  pride  which  just 
preceded  the  bursting  of  the  German  storm.  There  is 
therefore  something  curiously  piquant  in  the  fact  that 
*  Reinach,   "Apollo,"   p.   211. 


Raphael  207 

he  and  Luther  were  born  in  the  same  year.  The  famous 
cartoon  of  "Christ's  Appearance  to  His  Disciples  by  the 
Lake  of  Galilee  and  the  Charge  to  Peter,"  which  so 
aroused  the  wrath  of  Ruskin,  is  obviously  designed  to 
support  the  claims  of  the  Church.  "Note,"  says  Ruskin 
in  his  caustic  commentary,  "the  bold  fallacy — the  put- 
ting all  the  Apostles  there,  a  mere  lie  to  serve  the  papal 
heresy  of  the  Petric  supremacy,  by  putting  them  all  in 
the  background  while  Peter  receives  the  charge,  and 
making  them  all  witnesses  to  it."  Even  more  notably 
the  vast  religious,  historical,  and  allegorical  decorations 
of  the  stanse  and  loggie  of  the  Vatican,  undertaken  at 
the  command  of  Julius  II.  and  Leo  XM  have  as  one 
of  their  chief  objects  the  glorification  of  the  Roman 
power.  This  is  clearly  shown,  directly  or  symbolically, 
in  the  "Coronation  of  Charlemagne  "  and  the  "Donation 
of  Constantine"  (both  manifestoes  in  favour  of  the  Hilde- 
brandine  conception  of  the  Pope's  temporal  supremacy), 
the  "Checking  of  Attila  by  Leo  L,"  the  "Driving  of 
Heliodorus  from  the  Temple,"  the  "Deliverance  of 
Peter,"  the  "Fire  in  the  Borgo,"  and  the  "Triumph  of 
the  Church."  At  the  same  time  the  full  influence  of 
the  classical  revival  and  the  humanistic  enthusiasms 
of  the  leaders  of  the  papal  court  are  equally  apparent 
in  the  "  School  of  Athens "  and  the  "  Parnassus." 
Of  the  general  spirit  of  Raphael's  work  on  scriptural 
subjects,  it  may  perhaps  be  said  that  at  his  best  it 
exhibits  a  more  perfect  blending  than  can  be  found 
anywhere  else  in  Renaissance  art  of  real  devotional  V 
feeling  with  a  sense  of  beauty  and  consummate  J 
skill.  But  the  union  was  for  one  happy  moment 
only.  Even  in  Raphael's  own  later  productions  the- 
signs  of  approaching  decay  are  unmistakable;  while,- 
save  only  in  Venice,  deterioration  went  on  rapidly 
throughout  Italian  art  after  the  master's  death.     It  was 


208  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

not  that  the  painters  of  the  decline  lacked  science  or 
perception,  quickness  of  mind  or  dexterity  of  hand. 
They  were,  on  the  contrary,  extremely  clever  craftsmen, 
who  had  learned  all  the  technical  lessons  of  the  schools, 
worked  with  astonishing  dexterity  in  the  production  of 
their  effects,  and  understood  how  to  imitate  the  external 
qualities  of  style  of  their  great  forerunners.  But  the 
world  to  which  they  belonged  knew  nothing  of  moral 
idealism  or  lofty  emotion,  and  thus  at  best  there  was 
nothing  in  their  surroundings  to  inspire  them  with 
worthy  aims,  while  the  general  corruption  of  the  society 
about  them  inevitably  tended  to  the  debasement  of  their 
art.  Much  of  their  work  was  done  for  wealthy  patrons 
for  whom  art  had  become  a  luxury  and  the  means  of 

(vulgar  display,  and  whose  peculiar  tastes  it  was,  of 
course,  their  business  to  gratify.  Vast  and  showy 
pictures  on  historical,  mythological,  and  allegorical 
themes  were  in  special  demand — pictures  grandiose  in 
conception  and  bombastic  in  execution,  but  wholly 
wanting  in  nobility  of  purpose  and  depth  of  thought; 
and  a  common  feature  of  these  was  the  constant  abuse 

'  of  the  nude,  which,  serenely  pure  in  the  best  classic 
sculpture,  and  a  subject  of  scientific  interest  in  the  hands 

,  of  Michelangelo,  was  now  degraded  to  sensual  ends. 
Along  with  moral  vigour  and  sincerity  even  technical 
vitality  disappeared  from  art,  which,  like  the  architec- 
ture of  the  same  period,  became  more  and  more  cold, 

.  formal,  and  academic.  Sensationalism  and  the  love  of 
startling  effects  meanwhile  invaded  the  domain  of 
religious  art,  the  vicious  changes  in  the  tone  and  manner 
of  which  may  in  large  measure  be  traced  to  the  influ- 
ences of  the  Counter-reformation.  It  is  true  that  the 
movement  for  the  purification  of  life  which  grew  out  of 
this  helped  greatly  to  check  some  of  the  most  glaring 
evils  of  the  time ;  as,  for  example,  in  the  shameless  use 


Venetian  Painting  209 

of  the  nude.  Cardinal  Contarini,  whom  we  remember 
as  one  of  the  Catholic  reformers,  complained  that  in 
the  very  temple  of  God,  in  pictures  of  saints,  and  even 
in  altar-pieces,  there  were  scenes  clearly  designed  to 
excite  lascivious  desires ;  and  so  flagrant  did  the  abuse 
become  that  the  Council  of  Trent  was  compelled  to  take 
cognisance  of  it.  But  in  other  ways  the  influence  of 
the  Catholic  reaction  was  the  reverse  of  healthy.  In 
its  effort  to  recover  lost  ground  and  to  re-establish  its 
sway  over  men's  minds,  the  Church  gladly  employed 
all  the  means  at  its  disposal,  among  them  art.  But  the 
work  which  it  now  inspired  had  nothing  in  common  with 
the  severe,  dogmatic  productions  of  the  great  mediaeval 
painter-preachers.  The  aim  of  the  artist  was  to  stimu- 
late the  imagination  and  arouse  the  feelings  of  an  age  * 
which  had  grown  sceptical  and  jaded;  and  to  do  this 
he  had  recourse  now  to  excessive  sensibility,  now  to 
vulgar  and  even  repulsive  realism,  now  to  a  strange 
blending  of  sensuality  and  mysticism.  His  art,  in  a 
word,  became  theatrical.  Already  we  see  something  of 
this  spirit  in  Raphael's  contemporary,  Correggio,  who 
handles  his  Christian  and  his  pagan  motives  with  the 
same  luscious  grace  and  seductive  sentimentalism.  But 
its  full  effect  is  to  be  found  only  later,  as  in  the  cheap 
emotionalism  of  such  men  as  Guido  Reni  (1575-1642) 
and  Carlo  Dolci  (1516-1686). 

In  speaking  of  the  decline  of  Italian  painting  after 
Raphael's  death,  I  made  an  exception  in  favour  of 
Venice.  There,  where  it  developed  late,  art  continued 
to  flourish  in  all  the  fullness  of  its  power  down  to  the 
very  close  of  the  sixteenth  century — the  magnificent 
product  of  the  commercial  prosperity,  the  intellectual 
freedom,  and  the  civic  strength  of  the  City  of  the  Doges, 
and  the  marvellous  embodiment  of  its  material  and 
social  beauty,  its  love  of  pleasure  and  pageant,  its  lust 
o 


210  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

of  life.  Upon  the  splendour  and  charm  of  Venetian 
painting,  its  vitality,  its  variety,  its  wealth  of  radiant 
colour,  I  must  not  be  tempted  to  dwell.  The  one  thing 
we  have  to  note  about  it  is  the  spirit  by  which  it  was 
animated.  Venetian  painting  knew  nothing  of  mys- 
ticism, of  ecstatic  piety,  of  the  trials  and  aspirations  of 
the  soul.  The  austerity  and  other-worldliness  which  for 
a  time  had  been  prominent  elsewhere  in  art  it  had  always 
openly  repudiated.  It  was,  in  fact,  thoroughly  worldly 
and  profoundly  materialistic.  The  devotional  pictures 
of  Giovanni  Bellini  had  indeed  true  religious  feeling; 
but  "from  the  close  of  the  fifteenth  century  the 
Madonnas  and  the  saints  of  the  Venetian  painters  were 
no  longer  ascetic  and  morose  persons,  but  beautiful 
young  women  and  handsome  young  men,  with  bloom- 
ing complexions  and  sunny  hair,  who  loved  to  deck 
themselves  with  gorgeous  stuffs  and  held  life  to  be  well 
worth  living."*  In  the  great  decorative  pictures  of 
Carpaccio  (1466-1522),  full  of  characterisation,  realism, 
and  humour ;  in  the  work  of  Tintoretto,  with  its  amazing 
dramatic  vigour,  its  violent  action,  its  romantic  feeling, 
and  its  vivid  contrasts  of  light  and  shade;  in  Veronese's 
love  of  processions  and  pageantry,  which  makes  him 
turn  even  the  Marriage  at  Cana  into  an  immense 
crowded  festival,  we  have  the  dominant  notes  of  Vene- 
tian art.  We  have  them  again  in  its  greatest  master 
>and  completest  representative,  the  mighty  Titian,  who, 
equally  at  home  in  episodes  from  pagan  mythology  and 
in  subjects  belonging  to  the  traditional  range  of  Chris- 
tian art  (in  which  latter  he  exhibits  the  powerful  influ- 
ence of  the  Catholic  reaction),  stamps  everything  he 
touches  with  a  double  impress — the  impress  of  his  own 
genius  and  the  impress  of  the  spirit  of  his  time  and 
place.  More  than  any  other  Italian  school  of  painting, 
*  Reinach,  "Apollo,"  p.  172. 


Northern  Painting  211 

this  Venetian  school  represents  that  increasing  interest 
alike  in  man  and  in  nature  which  came  with  the  Renais- 
sance ;  more  than  any  other,  it  stands  for  the  life  of  the 
prfesent  world  and  the  absolute  secularisation  of  art. 


V, — RENAISSANCE    ART   IN   GERMANY    AND   THE   LOW 
COUNTRIES 

Thus  far  I  have  written  exclusively  of  Italian  art  as 
the  one  great  and  representative  art  of  our  period. 
Turning  now  to  art  outside  Italy,  I  need  touch  upon 
such  points  only  as  are  of  special  interest  in  connecting 
this  particular  subject  with  the  general  history  of  the 
Renaissance. 

In  Germany  and  the  Low  Countries  painting  was 
long  under  the  sway  of  the  Byzantine  school,  the 
tyranny  of  which  was  first  challenged  about  the  middle 
of  the  thirteenth  century.  Then  art,  passing  from  the 
hands  of  the  monks  into  those  of  the  laity,  began  to 
respond  to  the  stir  of  secular  life,  to  free  itself  from 
the  restraints  of  the  cloister,  and  even  to  seek  fresh 
material  in  romantic  legend.  Religious  subjects  none 
the  less  continued  to  provide  the  chief  part  of  the 
painter's  stock-in-trade,  though  in  his  treatment  of 
these  he  differed  considerably  from  his  Italian  contem- 
poraries; a  love  of  nature  and  of  homely  and  familiar 
detail,  a  wholesome  feeling  for  common  things,  and  a 
tendency  to  a  coarse  kind  of  truthfulness  being  among 
the  salient  features  of  his  work.  The  characteristic 
qualities  of  German,  Flemish,  and  Dutch  art  are  thus 
clearly  indicated.  Exceptions  there  were,  of  course; 
but,  speaking  in  general  terms,  we  may  say  that  these 
northern  masters  had  from  the  first  a  well-marked  lean- 
ing towards  actual  life  and  the  world  of  everyday  fact; 
that,  pious  and  sentimental  as  they  often  were,   they 


212  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

were  rarely  inspired  by  mystical  passion ;  and  that  the 
romantic  bias  of  northern  genius  was  shown  in  their 
free  use  not  only  of  the  commonplace,  but  even  of  the 
grotesque  in  the  handling  of  sacred  themes.  No  one 
would  seek  to  deny  the  genuine  religious  fervour  of 
the  Van  Eycks;  of  Martin  Schongauer  (1450-88);  of 
Hans  Memling  (1430-94),  whose  pictures  are  indeed 
conspicuous  for  their  devout  feeling ;  of  Memling's 
master,  Rogier  van  der  Weyden  (1400-64),  whose  sombre 
and  gloomy  spirit  finds  expression  in  the  agony  of 
tortured  saints  and  the  sufferings  of  Christ ;  of  Quentin 
Matsys  (1466-1530),  whose  frequent  vulgarity  of  detail 
does  not  obscure  the  sincerity  of  his  emotion.  Yet, 
comparing  the  work  even  of  these  men  with  that  of  the 
greatest  Italian  religious  painters,  we  can  hardly  fail 
to  perceive  both  their  larger  intellectual  independence 
and  their  relative  want  of  idealising  power.  The 
"tendency  to  give  poetry  to  realities  rather  than  to 
realise  a  conventional  ideal  is  prominent  throughout  the 
whole  course  of  Flemish  art,"  writes  Reinach ;  and  what 
he  says  about  Flemish  art  may  be  fairly  extended  to 
northern  art  in  general.  "Painters  were  obliged  to 
paint  sacred  pictures,  Virgins,  angels,  and  martyrs, 
because  their  clients  asked  for  these;  but  how  clearly 
they  show  that  all  of  them,  even  Memling,  would  gladly 
have  painted  anything  else !  The  things  that  interest 
them,  that  they  study  and  render  most  lovingly,  are 
figures  of  donors,  rich  stuffs,  distant  glimpses  of  land- 
scape. They  are  never  so  great  as  when  they  escape 
from  the  bondage  of  the  given  theme."*  The  common 
practice,  here  just  alluded  to,  of  introducing  the  por- 
traits of  donors,  patrons,  and  wealthy  burghers  in 
pictures  of  the  Holy  Family  and  similar  scenes,  is  a 
curious  illustration  of  the  downright  realistic  character 
*  "  Apollo,"  p.  227. 


Protestantism  and  Art  213 

of  this  northern  art.  In  many  ways,  as  will  be  seen, 
such  art  exhibits  the  workings  of  the  practical  and  posi- 
tive spirit  which  found  its  principal  expression  in 
Protestantism. 

We    are   thus   brought   to   a   question    of    primary 
interest  in  this  part  of  our  study — that  of  the  general^, 
influence  of  the  Reformation  on  art.     To  all  forms  of',1 
ecclesiastical  art  Protestantism  was  necessarily  hostile.  l 
It  is  true  that  in  this  matter  there  were  differences  of  , 
opinion  among  the  leaders.     Luther  himself  defended 
the  use  of  art  for  devotional  purposes,  both  because  it 
aided  the  memory  and   because  it   helped  to  a  vivid 
imaginative  realisation  of  incidents  of  sacred  story.    "If  * 
it  is  not  sinful,  but  good  and  holy,  that  I  should  have 
Christ's  image  in  my  heart,"  he  said,  "why  should  it  be 
sinful  for  me  to  have  it  in  my  eyes  ?  "     On  the  other 
hand,  Zwingli  and  his  followers  denounced  all  religious 
art  in  unmeasured  terms  as  an  invention  of  Anti-Christ 
— "the    Lord,"    they    declared,    "had    commanded    to 
preach  the  gospel,  not  to  paint  it";  and  the  Helvetian 
Confession  of  Faith  contained  an  express  denunciation^ 
of  all  images  as  idols.     On  the  whole,  the  judgment  of 
the  reformers  went  with  the  Swiss  party  rather  than 
with  Luther,  and  it  was  their  uncompromising  spirit 
which  became  the  controlling  influence  m  defining  the  i 
unfortunate  attitude  of  Protestantism  towards  art.     We^ 
need  not  make  too  much  of  the  disgraceful  outbursts  of 
fanaticism  which  occasionally  occurred;  as  when,   ac- 
cording to  the  report  given  by  Erasmus  to  Pirkheimer, 
a  frenzied  mob  laid  violent  hands  on  the  treasures  of 
the  Basel  churches,  obliterated  with  whitewash  all  the 
paintings  which  could  not  be  removed,  smashed  all  the 
sacred  images,  and  committed  to  the  flames  everything 
that  would  burn.    Such  iconoclastic  madness,  specific- 
ally condemned  by  Luther,  was  the  inevitable  product 


214  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

of  the  angry  passions  of  an  age  in  which,  as  we  saw  in 
an  earlier  chapter,  there  was  no  hope  for  counsels  of 
moderation  on  one  or  the  other  side.  It  is  not  with  such 
passing  gusts  of  unreasoning  fury  that  we  have  here 
to  reckon,  but  with  the  bearings  of  reformed  Christianity 
upon  the  evolution  of  art  at  large.  By  its  formal 
repudiation  of  the  use  of  beauty  in  the  service  of  reli- 
gion, Protestantism .  practically  "destroyed  ecclesiastical 
j&rt.  Of  the  disastrous  consequences  which  this  ill- 
advised  procedure  entailed  upon  its  own  destinies  we 
have  not  here  to  speak.  We  have  only  to  note  that  in 
this  way  aesthetic  genius,  now  completely  liberated  from 
sacerdotal  control,  gained  absolute  freedom  in  the 
treatment  of  sacred  subjects,  while  at  the  same  time  it 
was  greatly  encouraged,  indeed  almost  driven,  to  seek 
fresh  secular  channels  for  its  expression. 

The  history  of  the  immediate  influence  of  Protes- 
tantism upon  painting  is  not  very  interesting.  Among 
the  most  important  artists,  the  German,  Lucas  Cranach 
(1472-1553),  shows  it  most  conspicuously.  Cranach  was 
an  intimate  friend  of  Luther  and  Melanchthon,  and  in 
many  of  his  church  pictures  he  gives  his  compositions 
a  distinctly  dogmatic  and  polemical  turn.  Thus,  for 
example,  in  his  great  altar-piece  in  the  Stadtkirche  at 
Weimar,  he  depicts  Christ  on  the  cross  with  the  blood 
flowing  from  His  side,  while  John  the  Baptist  directs 
the  attention  of  Luther,  Melanchthon,  and  the  painter 
himself  to  the  suffering  Saviour  as  representing  the 
work  of  redemption.  Here,  clearly,  we  have  an  attempt 
to  make  art  the  embodiment  of  the  central  Protestant 
doctrine  of  the  Atonement.  In  the  productions  of  two 
much  greater  men,  the  spirit  of  the  reformed  religion, 
though  not  so  pronounced,  is  still  unmistakable. 
Albrecht  Diirer  (1471-1528),  the  most  famous  of  German 
masters,  painted  and  etched  at  Niirnberg  at  a  time  when 


Diirer  and  Holbein  215 

.  that  city  was  the  centre  of  German  humanism,  and  the 
f  impulses  of  the  intellectual  revival  must  be  counted 
;  among  the  formative  factors  in  the  development  of  his 
I  art.  It  was  during  his  later  years,  however,  that, 
'  though  he  never  severed  his  connection  with  the  Roman 
communion,  his  sympathy  with  the  Reformation  be- 
came pronounced.  Thus,  while  his  vast  allegorical 
picture  of  the  "Adoration  of  the  Trinity  "  is  executed  on 
conventional  lines  and  is  thoroughly  mediaeval  in  its 
balanced  representation  of  the  Church  and  the  Empire, 
his  late  masterpieces  of  the  "Four  Apostles  "  (St.  Peter, 
St.  John,  St.  Mark,  and  St.  Paul)  is  evidently  inspired 
by  interest  in  the  return  of  Christianity  to  its  apostolic 
sources,  the  appended  texts  from  Luther's  translation 
of  the  Epistles  serving  to  underscore  the  artist's  mean- 
ing. His  sacred  woodcuts  also  show  Protestant  ten- 
dencies in  their  free  treatment  of  their  subjects,  and 
especially  of  their  figures.  In  his  younger  contem- 
porary, Hans  Holbein.  (1497-1543),  the  power  of  Pro- 
testantism is  even  more  marked.  Detailed  examination 
of  his  religious  pictures,  which  are  not  numerous, 
would  show  this  in  the  sound  common  sense,  the  intel- 
lectual solidity,  and  the  homely  realism  by  which  they 
are  characterised,  while  occasionally,  as  in  some  of  the 
illustrations  to  Luther's  Bible,  and  in  drawings  of  the 
Passion  in  which  Christ's  judges,  accusers,  and  execu- 
tioners are  represented  under  the  guise  of  pope,  monks, 
and  priests,  the  dogmatic  purpose  of  the  artist  becomes 
aggressive.  We  have  not  at  the  moment  anything  to 
do  with  the  varied  output  of  both  Diirer  and  Holbein 
on  other  than  sacred  subjects.  Two  points  suggested 
by  their  work  in  general  may  still,  however,  be  empha- 
sised. In  the  first  place,  there  is  its  essentially  Teutonic 
character.  Protestant  in  temper  as  they  were,  they  were 
as  Germans  nearer  in  certain  ways  to  the  Middle  Ages 


216  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

than  their  Italian  contemporaries;  for  along  with  their 
downright  and  practical  temper  and  their  lack  of  mystical 
» passion,  there  is  a  large  infusion  in  the  productions  of 
» both  of  them  of  the  weird,  the  grotesque,  the  superstiti- 
ous, and  the  sentimental.  We  see  this  in  "The  Knight, 
Death,  and  the  Lady,"  and  the  "Melancholia"  of  the 
one,  and  in  the  "  Dance  of  Death"  of  the  other.  Secondly, 
we  should  recognise  the  significance  of  the  immense 
development  of  etching  and  engraving  not  in  the  hands 
of  these  two  masters  only,  but  in  northern  art  at  large. 
>  In  this  we  have,  socially  considered,  a  further  extension 
of  the  influence  of  the  oil  painting.  In  Italy,  as  we 
have  seen,  painting  as  it  ceased  to  be  mainly  ecclesiasti- 
cal tended  to  become  a  mere  luxury  of  the  very  rich. 
It  is  a  sign  of  the  different  spirit  bred  by  Protestantism 
among  the  northern  peoples  that  by  means  of  etching 
and  engraving  even  the  greatest  artists — even  men  like 
Diirer  and  Rembrandt — appealed  to  the  masses  as  well 
as  to  the  wealthy  and  cultivated  few;  and  in  this  case, 
again,  the  taste  of  the  public  affected  the  work  of  the 
artist.  Art  shared  with  religion  in  the  democratic  in- 
fluences of  which  Protestantism  was  at  once  part  cause 
and  part  effect. 

The  difference  between  Counter-reformation  and 
Reformation  in  their  respective  bearings  on  art  may 
be  further  illustrated  from  the  contrast  presented  by  the 
two  supreme  painters  of  the  seventeenth  century — the 
Flemish  Rubens  (1577-1649)  and  the  Dutch  Rembrandt 
(1616-69).  Both  these  great  masters  were  enormously 
industrious,  prolific,  and  versatile;  both  covered  almost 
,  the  entire  range  of  artistic  motives  possible  at  their  time, 
producing  portraits  and  landscapes,  allegorical  com- 
positions, and  pictures  on  subjects  from  history, 
religion,  mythology,  and  social  life.  But  Rubens  be- 
longed to  the  Spanish  Netherlands,  where  the  spirit  of 


Rubens  and  Rembrandt  217 

the  Counter-reformation  was  very  strong,  and  of  that 
spirit  he  is  in  his  religious  productions  the  most  brilliant 
interpreter.  Ruskin  speaks  of  him  as  a  man  "without 
any  perceptible  traces  of  a  soul."  The  want  of  soul,  the 
absence  of  real  spirituality,  is  a  conspicuous  character- , 
istic  of  his  work.  He  is  marvellously  effective;  but  in  a 
florid  and  sensational  way.  His  religious  paintings  are, 
in  fact,  often  vulgar,  not  on  account  of  any  triviality  in 
their  conception  or  of  the  introduction  of  commonplace 
details,  but  because  they  are  over-emphatic  and  theatri- 
cal; as  in  the  famous  "Crucifixion"  and  the  equally 
famous  "Descent  from  the  Cross,"  the  one  in  the 
Museum,  the  other  in  the  cathedral,  at  Antwerp.  Thus 
Rubens  exhibits,  more  fully  than  any  other  painter,  the 
spirit  which  the  Counter-reformation  had  brought  into 
art.  The  tone  of  Rembrandt's  sacred  pictures,  on  the  ' 
other  hand,  is  thoroughly  and  unmistakably  Protestant. 
Their  downright,  vigorous  naturalism  and  freedom  from  * 
convention  betray  their  inspiration.  In  his  "Descent 
from  the  Cross,"  for  instance,  there  are  many  grotesque 
touches,  and  the  Walloon  costumes  of  the  figures  pro- 
duce a  strange  effect;  but  here,  as  elsewhere,  the  artist's 
touch  of  familiarity  and  his  homely  truth  bring  his 
subjects  very  near  indeed  to  our  imagination  and 
sympathies. 

In  Germany  art  was  early  swamped  by  Italian  influ- 
ences, and  about  the  middle  of  the  sixteenth  century 
ceased  to  nourish  itself  from  the  soil'  of  national  life, 
gave  itself  up  to  imitation  of  foreign  models,  and  thus, 
becoming  devitalised  and  academic,  passed  rapidly  into 
decline.  Something  of  the  same  fate  later  overtook  the 
more  ambitious  forms  of  art  in  the  Netherlands, 
though  the  power  of  a  few  great  masters  saved  even 
these  for  a  time  from  complete  destruction.  The  really 
living  developments  of  northern  art  after  this  time  must, 


218  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

however,  be  sought  not  in  paintings  on  a  grand  scale 
and  on  religious  and  historical  themes,  but  rather  in 
little  pictures  for  private  houses  and  public  halls,  con- 
taining portraits,  landscapes,  domestic  interiors,  and 
transcripts  from  ordinary  life.  It  was  in  this  realistic 
work,  and  particularly  in  what  is  known  as  genre- 
painting,  that  much  of  the  best  genius  of  the  north  in 
the  later  Renaissance  period  found  its  expression,  and 
it  is  noteworthy  that  while  such  realistic  work  was  a 
thoroughly  characteristic  product  of  the  spirit  of  the 
Low  Countries  in  general,  it  developed  less  rapidly  and 
less  successfully  in  Catholic  Flanders  (and  this  notwith- 
standing the  prominence  of  David  Teniers)  than  in 
Protestant  Holland.  Dutch  art  was  born  along  with 
Dutch  freedom  in  that  great  hour  when  the  people 
of  Holland  finally  broke  the  yoke  and  discarded  the 
religion  of  their  Spanish  oppressors;  and  whatever  else 
may  be  said  about  it,  either  in  praise  or  in  blame,  this 
at  least  must  be  said — that  it  has  all  the  vitality  and 
the  meaning  which  only  a  genuine  national  art  can 
ever  possess.  It  is  not  an  ideal  art;  not  an  art  of  high 
emotion  or  intense  passion ;  it  has  no  spirituality ;  it  is 
little  concerned  with  the  great  things  of  religion,  or 
history,  or  experience.  It  is  just  the  art  of  nature  in 
its  common  aspects,  of  homely  people  and  their  doings, 
of  life  as  it  is  lived  day  by  day  through  quiet  uneventful 
years.  In  this  art  the  realism  which  had  long  been 
struggling  elsewhere  for  separate  existence  at  length 
becomes  entirely  independent.  Seeking  its  themes  and 
inspiration  in  the  range  of  simple  fact,  this  Dutch  art 
then  has  its  importance  as  representing  in  its  peculiar 
and  fruitful  fashion  one  phase  of  that  general  return  of 
the  mind  and  interests  of  men  to  the  world  and  reality 
which  we  have  found  a  chief  factor  in  the  movements 
of  the  Renaissance  as  a  whole. 


CHAPTER   VIII 
The  Renaissance  in  Literature 

I. — GENERAL    CHARACTERISTICS    OF    RENAISSANCE 
LITERATURE 

I  have  now  to  outline  the  development  of  literature 
during  the  period  of  the  Renaissance,  and,  as  in  the  pre- 
ceding sketch  of  art,  so  again  here,  we  are  to  concern 
ourselves  not  with  technical  details,  or  with  individual 
men  as  such,  or  with  the  lasting  value  of  what  they  did, 
but  with  general  movements  and  their  meanings.  What 
we  have,  then,  to  undertake  is  not  a  history,  but  an 
epitome,  and  the  aim  of  this  epitome  must  be  to  exhibit 
the  connection  between  literature  and  other  activities  of 
the  time.  That  Renaissance  literature,  like  Renaissance 
art,  is  charged  with  all  the  passion  and  energy  of  the 
great  age  out  of  which  it  sprang  is  a  fact  too  familiar  to 
every  reader,  even  to  the  English  reader  who  never 
travels  beyond  his  own  language,  to  need  repeated 
emphasis.  It  is  with  the  special  ways  in  which  literature 
was  affected  by  the  influences  which  we  have  been 
considering  that  we  now  have  to  do. 

A  natural  plan  would  be  to  follow  the  main  lines  of 
literary  evolution  as  we  previously  followed  the  course 
of  classical  scholarship,  from  Italy,  the  cradle  of 
humanism,  across  the  Alps  to  France,  and  thence  to 
England.  But  while  this  plan  would  doubtless  have  its 
advantages,  we  shall,  I  think,  economise  space  and  best 
subserve  our  purpose  by  dealing  with  our  subject  under 

219 


220  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

the  headings  rather  of  movements  than  of  countries. 
It  is  not  primarily  with  Italian  literature,  or  with  French 
literature,  or  with  English  literature  that  our  business 
now  lies.    It  is  with  the  qualities  and  tendencies  of  Re- 
naissance literature  in  general.     In  taking  the  proposed 
course  we  shall  find  that  all  the  literatures  of  the  new  age 
have  many   points  of   resemblance  one  with  another. 
Products  of  the  powerful  secularising  spirit  of  the  time, 
they  are  all  alike  characterised  by  reaction  from  sacerdo- 
talism and  the  cloistral  temper,  by  their  wide  interest  in 
the  present  world,  by  their  ardent  pursuit  of  fresh  ex- 
perience and  their  absorbing  delight  in  life  and  beauty. 
Whatever  else  this  Renaissance  literature  is,  it  is  cer- 
tainly a  literature  of  the  lay  mind,  of  free  inquiry,  of 
awakened  sensibilities,  of  intellectual  emancipation  and 
expansion.  Impulses  long  checked,  desires  long  banned, 
find  full  expression  in  it,  and  it  is  everywhere  quickened 
by  the  fertilising  ideas  by  which  men's  imaginations 
were  filled.     It  is,  moreover,  an  embodiment  not  only 
of  man's  newly  stirred  interest  in  the  outer  world,  but 
also  of  his  newly  stirred  interest  in  himself.     The  in- 
dividualism of  the  age  is  one  of  its  persistent  notes. 
Once  more,  almost  universally  in  its  forms,  often  in  its 
content  and  spirit,  it  reveals  the  influences  of  the  new 
learning  and  the  revival  of  classical  antiquity — influ- 
ences which  were  indeed  so  profound  and  far-reaching, 
for  both  good  and  evil,  that  from  the  purely  artistic 
point  of  view  they  may  be  justly,  as  they  are  popularly, 
regarded  as  the  chief  factors  in  literary  development. 
Inspired  by  the  same  interests  and  shaped  by  the  same 
forces,  the  literatures  of  the  Renaissance  thus  present, 
as  we  should  expect,  many  features  in  common.    Yet 
because,   broadly  viewed,   such   literatures  are  funda- 
mentally European,  they  do  not  therefore  cease  to  be 
Italian,   French,   and   English.      Essential  similarities 


Secularisation  of  Literature  221 

thus,  as  we  should  also  expect,  coexist  with  many  dif- 
ferences, and  as  these  differences  were  due  to  differ- 
ences in  national  character,  situation,  social  and  moral 
conditions,  and  the  varying  power  and  relationships  of 
the  impulses  involved,  they  will  prove  to  be  by  no  means 
the  least  important  point  in  the  comparative  inquiry 
which  we  have  now  to  undertake. 


II. — THE   SECULARISATION   OF   LITERATURE 

It  has  been  pointed  out  that  the  development  of  a 
large  body  of  miscellaneous  and  entirely  secular  literature 
was  one  of  the  principal  manifestations  of  the  working  of 
the  Renaissance  spirit.  That  there  was  no  secular  litera- 
ture during  the  Middle  Ages  is  not,  of  course,  implied. 
A  good  deal  existed,  as  especially  in  the  aristocratic 
chansons  de  gestes  and  romans,  and  in  the  more  popular 
songs,  ballads,  and  fabliaux.  But,  on  the  whole,  and 
even  outside  the  field  of  directly  religious  effort,  litera- 
ture was  still  hampered  by  theology  and  encumbered 
by  scholastic  metaphysics.  Deflected  by  these,  it  lost 
itself  too  often  in  the  dreary  wastes  of  didacticism, 
allegory,  abstractions,  and  personifications.  Its 
gradual  emergence  from  these  under  the  influence  of 
the  tendencies  which  culminated  in  the  Renaissance, 
represents  the  return  to  life  and  nature,  and  the  corre- 
sponding growth  of  an  interest  in  art  as  art.  Its  com- 
plete secularisation  meant  its  final  liberation  from 
ecclesiastical  and  scholastic  control,  its  escape  from  the 
atmosphere  and  the  prepossessions  of  cloister  and  class- 
room, the  assertion  of  its  right  to  independent  existence, 
its  claim  to  absolute  freedom  in  its  treatment  of  its  two 
great  subjects — the  world  and  man.  Thus,  for  example, 
story-literature  in  clerical  hands  had  been  an  appendage 
to  the  literature  of  edification;  moral  tags  (as  even  in 


222  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

the  "Gesta  Romanorum")  had  been  freely  used  to  justify 
amusement;  epical  narrative  had  been  continually  bur- 
dened with  allegory.  When  Boccaccio  and  Chaucer 
wrote  tales  which  frankly  depended  upon  their  interest 
as  tales — as  records  of  incidents,  transcripts  from  the 
human  comedy,  or  excursions  into  the  wonderland  of 
romance — they  stood  out  as  pioneers  of  that  coming 
literature  which  by  its  varied  dealings  with  fact  and 
fancy  was  to  appeal  directly  to  the  imagination  and 
sympathies  of  its  readers.  It  will  be  seen  that  to  a 
certain  extent  the  history  of  literature  paralleled  that 
of  art;  but  for  various  reasons  literature  gained  a  full- 
ness of  freedom  which  for  the  time  being  art  was  unable 
to  attain. 

Conspicuous  among  the  many  illustrations  of  this 
secularisation  of  literature  is  the  establishment  of  the 
modern  form  of  drama.  Whatever  traditions  of  the 
ancient  theatre  may  have  survived  the  disintegration  of 
the  Roman  Empire,  it  seems  certain  that  these  can  have 
had  little  or  nothing  to  do  with  the  beginnings  of  the 
drama  in  mediaeval  Europe.  That  drama  was  a  direct 
offshoot  from  religious  ceremonial.  It  arose  in  the  first 
instance  out  of  the  rich  symbolic  liturgy  of  the  mediaeval 
Church  by  the  gradual  dramatisation  of  important 
incidents,  especially  those  connected  with  Easter 
and  Christmas,  commemorated  in  the  services  of  the 
calendar.  In  its  original  form  of  liturgical  mystery,  it 
was,  in  fact,  an  organic  part  of  such  services,  and  thus 
we  are  speaking  without  the  slightest  extravagance 
when  we  say  that  the  first  stage  of  mediaeval  Europe 
was  the  altar,  and  the  first  actors  the  priests  engaged  in 
celebrating  the  mass.  The  expansion  of  this  liturgical 
mystery  was  necessarily  followed  in  due  course  by  its 
separation  from  the  ritual  and  its  removal  from  the 
church;  and,  ultimately,  a  great  religious  drama  evolved 


Evolution  of  the  Drama  223 

out  of  it,  the  subjects  of  which  were  taken  from  the 
Old  and  the  New  Testaments  and  the  lives  of  the  saints, 
and  the  central  purpose  of  which  was  to  educate  the 
illiterate  people  in  the  truths  of  their  religion.  As  a 
means  of  securing  a  concrete  and  vivid  representation  of 
sacred  things,  the  growth  of  this  religious  drama,  there- 
fore, falls  into  line  with  the  development  of  religious 
painting,  stained-glass  windows,  and  (as,  for  instance, 
in  the  occasional  use  of  tableaux)  other  external  aids  to 
faith.  The  substitution  of  the  vernacular  language  for 
the  ecclesiastical  Latin  originally  employed,  and  the  dis- 
placement— which,  however,  for  a  time  went  on  very 
slowly — of  clerical  by  lay  actors,  are  signs  of  the 
encroachment  of  secular  influences  upon  a  domain 
which,  to  begin  with,  was  entirely  ecclesiastical. 
Already  popular  in  many  parts  of  the  Continent,  these 
mysteries  and  miracle  plays  were  imported  into  England 
after  the  Norman  Conquest;  the  earliest  play  performed 
in  this  country  of  which  any  record  has  been  preserved, 
being  a  "Ludus  de  S.  Katherina,"  which  was  repre- 
sented at  Dunstable  at  the  end  of  the  eleventh  or  the  be- 
ginning of  the  twelfth  century.  A  hundred  years  later 
such  representations  were  common  all  over  the  country. 
But  the  great  age  of  the  English  religious  drama  dawned 
with  the  fourteenth  century,  when  Corpus  Christi 
plays,  as  they  were  called  from  the  season  of  their 
performance  at  the  great  festival  of  that  name,  became 
popular  in  many  of  our  larger  towns.  One  curious 
feature  in  their  development  at  this  time  merits  atten- 
tion. From  the  outset  the  tendency  of  the  religious 
drama  had  been  towards  expansion  by  the  continual 
incorporation  of  more  and  more  fresh  matter  with  the 
few  original  themes.  In  England  this  tendency 
resulted  in  the  production  of  immense  sequences  or 
cycles  of  pjays — Collective  Mysteries,  as  they  are  some- 


224  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

times  termed — which,  Beginning  with  the  Creation  and 
ending  with  Doomsday,  provided  a  fairly  systematic 
treatment,  in  terms  of  the  current  theology,  of  the  central 
■j/  problems  of  sin  and  salvation.*  As  these  plays  had 
now  passed  into  the  hands  of  the  trade-guilds  of  the 
different  towns,  the  actual  clerical  monopoly  of  the 
drama  was  completely  destroyed.  Many  other  in- 
fluences were  meanwhile  at  work  helping  to  widen  the 
breach  between  the  Church  and  the  drama.  All  this  is 
significant.  But  still  more  significant  is  the  tendency 
of  the  religious  play  to  outrun  its  first  dogmatic,  and 
still  stated,  purpose,  by  amplification  on  the  purely 
dramatic  side.  The  humorous  wrangling  of  Cain  and 
his  boy,  and  of  Noah  and  his  wife,  and,  most  of  all,  the 
two  Shepherd  Plays  in  the  Wakefield  cycle — which  are 
simply  rough,  north-country,  rustic  farces,  very  slightly 
connected  with  their  matrix — show  how,  even  while  still 
working  within  the  narrow  frame  of  prescribed  subject- 
matter,  these  old  playwrights  and  actors  began  to  feel 
the  human  possibilities  of  what  they  had  in  hand,  and 
were  instinctively  seeking  a  way  towards  a  genuine  kind 
of  drama.  Their  experiments  thus  remind  us  of  the 
efforts  of  some  of  the  Italian  painters  towards  realistic 
expansion  under  the  cover  of  sacred  themes. 

This  religious  drama  had  a  long  history.  It  main- 
tained its  vogue  in  France  till  nearly  the  middle  of  the 
sixteenth  century;  it  developed  in  Italy  into  the  more 
artistic  form  of  the  fifteenth  century  rappresentazioni 
sacre,  and  in  Spain  into  the  autos  sacramentales,  of 
which  some  hundreds  of  examples  were  provided  by  the
