prolific  pens  of  Lope  de  la  Vega  and  Calderon ;  in  both 

*  Foux  of  these  cycles  have  come  down  to  us  complete :  the 
Chester  cycle  of  25  plays,  the  Coventry  of  42,  the  Wakefield  of  31, 
and  the  York  of  48.  The  great  Passion  Play  at  Ober-Ammergau  is 
our  modern  survival  and  representative  of  this  mediaeval  religious 
drama. 


Morality  Plays  225 

Germany  and  England  it  persisted  well  on  into  the 
period  of  the  Reformation,  the  theory  that  Protestantism 
was  directly  antagonistic  to  it  being  a  mistake.  Mean- 
while another  stage  in  dramatic  evolution  had  been 
marked  by  the  rise  of  the  morality  play,  which  was 
simply  the  translation  of  didactic  allegory  into  dramatic 
form.  This  fact  is  itself  enough  to  show  that  in  origin 
the  morality  was  as  mediasval  as  the  mystery.  Yet 
various  points  of  difference  between  the  two  classes  are 
suggestive.  In  the  first  place,  while  both  were  vehicles 
of  the  teachings  of  the  Church  on  questions  connected 
with  man's  salvation,  the  mystery,  as  M.  Jusserand  has 
admirably  said,  represented  the  more  popular  religious 
thought  of  the  middle  ages,  while  the  morality  ex- 
pounded its  scholastic  philosophy.  Secondly,  the 
morality  indicates,  in  a  most  interesting  way,  the 
movement  of  the  drama  towards  human  experience  , 
and  the  growth  of  the  element  of  individuality.  The 
mystery  had  dramatised  sacred  history,  and  had 
presented  the  teachings  of  the  Church  indirectly  through 
this.  In  the  morality  doctrine  was  turned  directly  into 
dramatic  form,  and  the  bearings  of  the  truths  involved 
were  enforced  by  the  action  and  dialogue  of  characters, 
who  were  indeed  personifications  of  virtues  and  vices, 
but  in  whom  the  spectator  might  well  perceive  enlarged 
types  of  the  humanity  of  which  he  was  himself  a  part. 
That  at  its  best — as  in  "Everyman,"  for  example — the 
morality  tended  to  bring  the  drama  down  towards  the 
plane  of  actual  life  cannot,  I  think,  be  denied.  Thus, 
though  it  is  true  that  in  its  later  developments  it  often 
became  hopelessly  pedantic,  it  is  also  true  that  in  other 
cases,  as  the  human  element  gained  ground  at  the 
expense  of  the  abstractions,  and  the  personified  qualities 
became  more  and  more  sharply  individualised,  it  passed 
insensibly  into  comedy,  being  indeed  one  of  the  affluents 


226         The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

by  which  early  comedy  was  fed.  Its  use  for  other  than 
its  original  purposes  is  also  an  interesting  feature  of  its 
history.  In  the  troubled  times  of  the  Reformation  it 
was  employed  by  both  Catholics  and  Protestants  as  a 
controversial  instrument.  It  was  also  turned  to  the  ser- 
vice of  the  new  learning,  such  a  play,  for  instance,  as  the 
"Interlude  of  the  Four  Elements" — the  earliest  of  the 
kind  that  has  come  down  to  us — being  not  a  dramatised 
sermon  or  homily,  but  a  lecture  on  physical  science  and 
its  value,  in  the  shape  of  a  drama  in  which  Natura 
Naturata,  Studious  Desire,  and  Humanity  are  the  chief 
speakers.  A  significant  detail  of  the  play  is  the  refer- 
ence in  it  to  the  discovery  of  America. 

The  early  history  of  the  modern  European  drama 
thus  shows  that  the  rise  of  the  regular  secular  drama  in 
the  sixteenth  century  was  not  so  sudden  an  occurrence 
as  is  sometimes  supposed.  The  oft-repeated  theory  that 
the  origin  of  modern  comedy  and  tragedy  has  to  be 
sought  entirely  in  "the  study  and  imitation  of  classical 
plays  in  schools  and  Universities  "*  is  quite  disproved 
by  the  fact  that  under  its  pre-secular  forms  a  steady 
movement  towards  the  secularisation  of  the  drama  is 
apparent  long  before  the  influences  of  the  classical 
revival  began  to  count.  Yet  it  would  not  be  easy  to 
exaggerate  the  stimulating  and  shaping  power  of  such 
influences  in  the  subsequent  course  of  the  European 
stage.  In  general,  as  we  have  seen,  the  classical  revival 
gave  an  enormous  impetus  to  the  spread  of  the  secular 
spirit;  and  of  that  spirit  the  new  drama  was  one  expres- 
sion. In  particular,  it  provided  the  rudimentary  drama 
with  much-needed  guidance  in  respect  of  technique  and 
form.  To  this  latter  point  we  will  return  presently.  In 
the  meanwhile  we  have  only  to  record  the  final  stage  of 
the  secular  evolution  of  the  drama  in  the  definite  estab- 
*  Henry  Morley,   "English  Plays,"   p.   2, 


Individualism  in  Literature  227 

lishment  of  regular  comedy  and  tragedy.  In  Italy 
comedy  may  be  said  to  begin  with  Cardinal  Bibbiena's 
"La  Calandria"  (written  probably  about  1490,  though 
not  performed  till  much  later),  Machiavelli's  "Mandra- 
gola"  (15 12),  and  the  five  comedies  of  Ariosto;  tragedy 
(freeing  itself  from  the  rappresentazioni  sacre)  with 
Tassoni's  "Sofonisba"  (1515).  In  France  the  founda- 
tions of  the  new  tragedy  and  the  new  comedy  alike  were 
laid  by  fitienne  Jodelle,  with  "Cteopatre"  (1552),  in  the 
one  case,  and  "Eugene"  (same  date)  in  the  other.  Real 
English  comedy  begins  with  Nicholas  Udall's  "Roister 
Doister "  (circa  1550),  and  John  Still's  "  Gammer 
Gurton's  Needle"  (circa  1566);  real  English  tragedy 
with  the  "Gorboduc"  of  Sackville  and  Norton  (1561-62). 
Thus,  in  a  type  of  art  which  had  grown  up  in  the  service 
of  theology  and  had  long  been  justified  by  its  doctrinal 
utility,  men  now  sought  humour  and  passion,  the 
interest  of  action  and  character,  beauty  of  form  and 
style- 


in. — THE  DEVELOPMENT  OF  INDIVIDUALISM  IN  LITERATURE 

In  these  pages  it  has  often  become  apparent  that  the 
spread  of  the  secular  spirit  during  the  period  of  the 
Renaissance  was  closely  associated  with  the  develop- 
ment of  the  sense  of  individuality,  and  ample  proof  has 
thus  been  provided  of  Burckhardt's  statement,  quoted 
at  the  outset,  that  the,  awakening  of  personality  was  the 
great  sign  of  the  new  time.  Additional  proof  is  now 
furnished  by  Renaissance  literature.  Mediaeval  anony- 
mity, upon  which  we  remarked  in  our  introductory 
chapter,  disappears;  the  individual  factor  comes  into 
full  play;  the  strong  impress  of  idiosyncrasy  and 
specific  experience  is  everywhere  conspicuous  in  the 
work  produced. 


228  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

The  expansion  of  personality  must  be  considered  in 
the  broadest  possible  way.  It  meant  much  more  than 
the  phrase  might  seem  at  first  to  connote.  As  man's 
great  aim  was  now  at  all  costs  to  be  himself,  it  meant 
self-realisation,  and  this  in  turn  implied  the  widening  of 
the  area  of  experience,  more  varied  contact  with  men  and 
things,  increasing  flexibility  and  breadth  of  outlook.  It 
meant,  therefore,  on  the  one  hand,  a  deepening  of 
interest  in  selfhood  and  its  shifting  phases  of  thought 
and  feeling,  and  a  growth  of  what  we  are  accustomed  to 
call  self-consciousness  or  subjectivity.  It  meant  equally 
on  the  other  hand  a  corresponding  deepening  of  interest 
in  the  characters  and  doings  of  other  people,  for  which 
reason  the  establishment  of  the  "cult  of  the  ego"  was 
accompanied  by  ever-extending  curiosity  concerning 
whatever  belongs  to  the  life  of  man.  These  are  some 
aspects  of  the  evolution  of  individuality  in  an  age  which 
had  discovered  at  once  both  the  inner  and  the  outer 
world;  and  it  is  worth  while  to  note  that  the  striking 
freshness  of  Renaissance  literature  is,  in  large  measure, 
the  result  rather  of  this  new-found  freedom  of  personal 
expression  than  of  the  actual  originality  of  the  ideas 
expressed. 

To  illustrate  the  individualism  of  that  literature  in 
detail  would  be  almost  tantamount  to  writing  its  history. 
This  obviously  cannot  be  attempted  here.  But  a  few 
outstanding  features  may  be  indicated. 

To  the  English  reader,  whose  ideas  on  the  subject 
are  naturally  coloured  by  his  familiarity  with  the 
Shakespearean  drama,  it  may  cause  some  surprise  to 
learn  that  one  of  the  most  conspicuous  characteristics  of 
the  European  literature  of  the  Renaissance  in  general  is 
its  prevailing  egotism.  It  is,  on  the  whole,  the  produc- 
tion of  men  who  are  immensely  concerned  with  them- 
selves.    A  careful  student  of  one  division  of  its  history, 


Autobiographical  Literature  229 

Mr,  Arthur  Tilley,  has  specially  noted  this  intimately 
personal  quality  in  the  literature  of  the  French 
Renaissance.  That  literature,  he  points  out,  is  essen- 
tially the  literature  of  self-portraiture;  its  writers  "talk 
about  themselves  not  because  they  think  they  are 
interesting  to  others,  but  because  they  are  supremely 
interested  in  themselves  " ;  he  calls  attention  to  the  fact 
that  "there  is  hardly  a  work  of  the  sixteenth  century, 
however  impersonal  in  form,  which  is  not  full  of  in- 
formation as  to  the  life  and  character  of  the  writer"; 
and  he  lays  stress  upon  the  autobiographical  material, 
not  only  in  the  lyrical  poetry  of  men  like  Marot,  Du 
Bellay,  and  Ronsard,  but  also  in  such  nominally  objec- 
tive books  as  Rabelais'  "  Pantagruel "  and  the  "Hep- 
tameron  "  of  the  Queen  of  Navarre.*  The  astonishing 
vogue  of  the  personal  memoir  in  the  French  literature 
of  the  time,  and  of  autobiographies  (of  which  those  of 
Cellini  and  Cardan  may  be  cited  as  examples)  in  Italian 
literature,  is  also  noteworthy.  Most  of  these  memoirs 
and  autobiographies  were  mainly  occupied  with  external 
facts.  But  a  strong  current  of  pure  subjectivity  had 
also  been  set  flowing  through  European  literature,  the 
themes  of  which  were  thus  occasionally  furnished  not 
by  outer  facts,  but  by  inner  experiences.  Dante's 
autopsychographical  "Vita  Nuova"  stands  in  the  fore- 
front of  this  literature  of  confessional  utterance,  but  it 
is  commonly  recognised  that  the  first  influential  ex- 
ponent of  the  mood  of  introspection  was  Petrarch,  in 
whose  sonnets  and  canzonieri  the  element  of  personal 
passion  is  so  prominent  that  he  has  even  been  described 
as  the  prototype  of  Rousseau  and  the  real  father  of  the 
modern  literature  of  self-analysis  and  self-delineation. f 
Petrarch's  influence  upon  European  poetry  was  enor- 

*  "  Literature  of  the  French  Renaissance,"  ii,  315. 
t  Garnett,   "History  of  Italian  Literature,"  p.  63. 


230  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

mous,  and  if  among  our  English  sonneteers  some,  like 
Spenser  in  his  "Amoretti,"  made  lyrical  verse  the 
medium  of  genuine  self-expression,  while  with  others, 
among  them  perhaps  Shakespeare,  the  sonnet-sequence 
was  only  a  dramatic  device,  still,  in  the  one  case  as  in 
the  other,  and  whether  the  feelings  were  real  or  feigned, 
the  dominant  interest  was  provided  by  the  complexities 
and  conflicts  of  the  emotional  life.  The  emergence  of 
the  essay — the  prose  analogue  of  the  lyric — in  the  hands 
of  Montaigne  and  Bacon  must  also  be  remarked.  The 
rambling  gossip  of  the  one,  and  the  "dispersed  medita- 
tions" of  the  other,  belong  to  the  literature  of  direct 
self-expression. 

Meanwhile  literature  responded  as  readily  to  the 
impulses  bred  of  that  widening  contact  with  general 
human  life  which,  as  I  have  shown,  was  only  another 
aspect  of  Renaissance  individualism.  Here,  again, 
Dante  is  the  pioneer,  for  while  the  plan  and  ideas  of  his 
"Comedy"  are  entirely  mediaeval,  the  "power  and  rich- 
ness shown  in  his  descriptions  of  human  nature  in  every 
shape  and  attitude"*  anticipate  a  later  time.  Growing 
interest  in  man  as  man,  and  especially  (a  significant 
detail)  in  strong,  outstanding  personalities,  gave  im- 
mense stimulus  to  the  literature  of  biography,  the  popu- 
larity of  which  may  be  compared  with  that  of  the 
portrait  in  painting.  This  was  particularly  the  case  in 
Italy,  where  not  only  innumerable  lives  of  individuals 
(like  Boccaccio's  "Life  of  Dante,"  which  led  the  way), 
but  also  voluminous  collections  (like  Vasari's  "Lives  of 
the  Painters,"  from  which  we  have  more  than  once 
quoted)  bore  testimony  to  the  power  of  the  current  taste. 
But  we  must  also  remember  that  this  interest  in  man 
as  man  found  embodiment  in  imaginative  no  less  than 
in  historic  forms.     For  reasons  into  which  we  must  not 

*  Burckhardt,  "  The  Renaissance  in  Italy,"  Part  IV.,  chap.  it. 


Montaigne  231 

now  pause  to  enter,  it  made  comparatively  little  head- 
way in  prose  fiction,  which,  from  the  point  of  view  of 
personality,  as  well  as  of  art,  was  greatly  retarded  in 
its  growth.  But  it  was  the  inspiring  motive  of  the  new 
drama.  That  drama  moved  along  the  lines  indicated 
by  the  morality-play,  from  types  and  abstractions  to 
individualities,  and  it  was  to  the  keen  sense  of  indivi- 
duality, of  the  varieties  of  experience,  of  the  boundless 
possibilities  of  human  nature  for  good  and  evil,  that  it 
made  its  appeal. 

Twice  already  in  previous  chapters,  and  a  third  time 
just  now,  I  have  had  occasion  to  refer  to  one  of  the 
greatest  writers  in  Renaissance  literature — the  French 
essayist  Montaigne,  whom  I  have  described  as  "the  very 
incarnation  of  Ihe  inquisitive  temper  of  his  age."  Re- 
presentative in  this  sense,  he  is  also  representative  on 
account  of  the  absolutely  personal  quality  of  his  writing; 
and  if  I  return  to  this  point  once  again  for  a  moment, 
it  is  not  so  much  because  he  was  admittedly  the  typical 
egotist  of  the  Renaissance,  as  it  is  because  it  is  most 
important  that  we  should  appreciate  the  real  nature  and 
bearings  of  his  egotism.  More  than  any  of  his  contem- 
poraries, he  combined  in  himself  the  two  phases  of  in- 
dividualism at  which  we  have  glanced — intense  interest 
in  himself  and  intense  interest  in  everything  connected 
with  man.  Individualism  is  thekey-note  of  his  "  Essays," 
To  the  last  degree  self-engrossed,  he  is  for  himself  the 
pivot  of  a  universe  of  infinite  variety  and  inexhaustible 
fascination.  "It  is  myself  I  portray";  "I  look  within 
myself,  and  have  no  business  but  with  myself";  "my 
book  is  consubstantial  with  its  author  ";  "my  book  and 
I  march  together  and  keep  one  pace";  "all  the  world 
may  know  me  by  my  book  " — in  such  terms  he  openly 
proclaims  his  constant  pre-occupation  with  himself,  his 
own  life,  character,  experiences,  aims,  opinions.     Indivi- 


232  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

dualism  is  also  the  key-note  of  his  moral  teaching.  "The 
greatest  thing  in  the  world  is  for  a  man  to  know  that 
he  is  his  own."  True  to  this  underlying  principle,  he 
throws  the  utmost  emphasis  upon  the  value  of  whatever 
a  man  can  build  into  the  fabric  of  his  life  to  its  amplifica- 
tion and  enrichment.  There  is  nothing  ascetic  about 
him;  he  is  not  afraid  of  experience,  nor  will  he  shirk  it. 
Yet  none  the  less — and  this  is  the  obverse  of  the  same 
thought — he  draws  the  sharpest  distinction  between  man 
and  his  circumstances — the  things  which  stand  outside 
him;  between  the  mere  trappings  and  accidents  of  life 
and  life  in  its  true  essence;  between  our  stage  costume 
and  our  skin.  "There  is  nothing  so  goodly  and  so  lawful 
as  to  play  the  man  well  and  duly.  Of  all  the  infirmities 
we  have,  the  most  savage  is  to  despise  our  being.  The 
true  husbandry  is  to  know  how  to  enjoy  it.  And  as  I 
now  perceive  my  life  to  be  short  in  time,  I  will  strive  to 
extend  it  in  weight."  The  wisdom  of  pure  egotism 
could  hardly  go  farther.  Montaigne's  very  inconsist- 
encies— and  they  were  innumerable  :  his  habit  of  taking 
one  view  of  a  subject  to-day  and  another  view  to- 
morrow; his  undogmatic  temper;  the  suppleness  of  his 
judgment;  his  far-reaching  scepticism;  his  uncompro- 
mising honesty  of  dealing  with  himself  as  well  as  with 
the  people  about  him — all  these  things  were  only  so 
many  manifestations  of  a  marvellously  alert  and  flexible 
mind  allowing  itself  at  all  times  absolute  freedom  of 
play.  Yet  such  preoccupation  with  himself  and  the 
conduct  of  his  own  life  was,  as  I  have  hinted,  only  one 
aspect  of  Montaigne's  individualism.  The  other  aspect 
of  it  is  to  be  found  in  his  wide  and  varied  contact  with 
the  world,  in  the  avidity  with  which  he  tested  life  on 
all  sides,  in  his  insatiable  curiosity,  in  his  tolerance  and 
comprehensive  sympathy.  In  the  words  of  one  of  his 
favourite    mottoes — a   phrase    from   Terence — he    was 


Classicism  and  Literature  233 

human,  and  nothing  human  did  he  deem  foreign  to  him. 
Hence  his  fondness  for  dwelling  upon  "diversity"  as 
"the  most  constant  quality  in  human  nature";  his  love 
of  travel;  his  devotion  to  history,  which  in  a  marvel- 
lously modern  spirit  he  studied  as  indeed  the  "natural 
history"  of  man.  It  is  not  paradoxical  to  say  that  his 
interest  in  himself  was  at  bottom  an  interest  in  himself 
as  a  specimen  of  mankind.  His  fundamental  revolt 
against  authority  and  the  dead  hand  of  the  past  was 
yet  another  expression  of  the  individualism  which  was 
the  basis  and  the  governing  principle  of  all  the  living 
and  all  the  thinking  of  this  "first  modern  man." 


IV. — THE  CLASSIC  REVIVAL  AND  LITERATURE 

While  considering  the  liberalising  tendencies  of 
humanism,  we  learned  how  much  the  spread  of  the 
secular  spirit  and  the  development  of  individualism  were 
stimulated  in  the  new  generation  by  the  contact  of  its 
mind  with  the  mind  of  classical  antiquity.  As  a  factor 
in  the  intellectual  and  moral  evolution  of  literature,  the 
rebirth  of  classical  antiquity  was  therefore  of  marked  im- 
portance. As  a  factor  in  its  artistic  evolution,  however, 
its  importance  is  even  greater.  So  obviously,  indeed, 
was  the  literature  of  the  Renaissance  fashioned  upon 
the  models  furnished  by  Greece  and  Rome,  so  much  did 
it  depend  upon  these  for  inspiration,  guidance,  and  pre- 
cedent, that  historians  of  narrow  outlook  have  made  the 
mistake  of  assuming  that  the  revival  of  literature  was 
entirely  the  result  of  the  revival  of  learning.  Even  the 
brief  survey  which  we  have  taken  in  this  book  of  the 
general  movements  which  combined  in  the  transforma- 
tion of  life  during  the  period  of  the  Renaissance  will 
have  sufficed  to  expose  the  radical  unsoundness  of  this 
view.     Yet,   as  we  shall  see,   the  direct   influence   of 


234  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

classicism  upon  the  forms  and  methods  of  literature 
was  so  profound  as  to  some  extent  to  account  for,  though 
not,  of  course,  to  excuse  it. 

That  influence  is  shown  at  once  and  most  broadly  in 
the  fact  that  literature  now  came  to  be  regarded  as  an 
art,  and  that  men  began  to  concern  themselves  about 
the  principles  and  rules  of  composition.  Elsewhere,  as 
we  have  noted,  the  classicaLi^vivaWeste^d^^he-iiiflQd 
of  inquiry  and  criticism,  and  the  extension  of  this  mood 
to  art  was  naturally  followed  by  the  growth  of  a  con- 
siderable body  of  literature  dealing  immediately  with 
literature  and  with  questions  of  technique.  This  did  not 
indeed  occur  in  the  earlier  years  of  the  revival,  when 
men  were  too  enthusiastic  over  their  recovered  treasures 
and  too  impatient  of  matters  of  detail  in  their  enjoyment 
of  them  to  pause  to  analyse,  weigh,  and  consider.  But  a 
race  of  more  sober  scholars  succeeded  that  of  the  early 
passionate  humanists,  and  then  criticism  began  in 
earnest.  The  "Ars  Poetica"  of  Vida — Pope's  "im- 
mortal Vida,  on  whose  honour'd  brow  the  poet's  bays 
and  critic's  laurel  grow" — published  in  1527,  was  the 
first  noteworthy  treatise  of  the  kind.  This  shows  the 
powerful  influence  of  Horace,  whose  own  "Ars  Poetica  " 
was  at  the  moment  the  great  textbook  of  criticism.  A 
little  later  Aristotle's  "Poetics"  came  to  the  front  and 
was  soon  the  standard  authority  in  literary  discussion. 
It  is  certainly  one  of  the  most  curious  coincidences  in 
literary  history  that  the  dictatorship  of  Aristotle  in 
poetry  should  have  been  firmly  established  at  the  very 
time  when  his  long  supremacy  in  philosophy  was  being 
fatally  undermined.  Italy  played  the  principal  part  in 
the  literature  of  criticism  during  the  sixteenth  century, 
after  which  the  leadership  in  opinion  passed  to  France. 
Here  already  the  ground  had  been  broken,  especially  by 
the  members  of  the  famous  Pleiade,  a  brotherhood  of 


* 


The  Growth  of  Criticism  235 

seven  sanguine  men  with  Ronsard  at  their  head,  who 
about  the  middle  of  the  sixteenth  century  banded  them- 
selves together  for  the  reform  of  the  French  language 
and  of  French  poetry.    They  had  had  their  forerunners, 
but  it  was  by  them  that  the  foundations  of  French 
criticism  were  permanently  laid.    Their  principal  mani- 
festo was  Du  Bellay's  treatise,  "La  Deffense  et  Illustra- 
tion de  la  Langue  Franfoyse,"  published  in  1549.     In 
this  the  extreme  classicism  of  the  school  is  very  clearly 
revealed,    for   the    writer    breaks    absolutely    with    the 
earlier    traditions   of   French    poetry,    "and   proclaims 
without  reservation  that  the  only  good  models  are  those 
of  Greece  and  Rome,  and  the  country  which  has  first 
been    quickened   by    the    Renaissance    spirit — Italy." 
The   distinct  classical  Bias   which  was  thus  given   to 
French  literature  is  historically  significant.     From  this 
time   onward   critical    literature   developed   rapidly   in 
France,  and  with  it  the  influence  of  Aristotle;  though 
it  should  be  noted  that  the  literary  principles  of  the  great 
Greek  thinker  were  now  as  much  perverted  by  his  dis- 
ciples as  his  philosophical  theories  had  formerly  been 
by  the  schoolmen.       It  was  about  the  middle  of  the 
sixteenth  century  also  that  criticism  definitely  emerged 
in   England;  and  in   English  treatises  on  the  formal 
aspects  of  literature — as  in  Webbe's  "Discourse  of  Eng- 
lish Poesie  "  (1586),  and  Puttenham's  "Arte  of  English 
Poetrie"  (1589),  and  Sidney's  "Apologie  for  Poetrie  " 
(circa  1581),  to  mention  three  of  the  most  important  ex- 
amples— the  paramount  influences  are  those  of  the  classics 
and  Italy.     But  our  native  playwrights  and  poets  were, 
on  the  whole,   impatient  of  the  schoolmaster  and  his 
counsels  of  restraint,  and  critical  theory  in  England  had 
much  less  to  do  with  the  actual  making  of  literature  than 
in  either  of  the  great  literary  countries  of  the  Continent. 
*  Tilley,  "The  Literature  of  the  French  Renaissance,"  i.  313. 


236         The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

Concurrently  with  the  growth  of  interest  in  litera- 
ture as  an  art  went  the  rapid  evolution  of  literary  forms; 
and  as  it  was  to  the  masterpieces  of  Greece  and  Rome 
that  the  men  of  the  new  age  turned  for  their  rules  of 
judgment  and  standards  of  taste,  so  it  was  in  these  also 
that  they  sought  precedent  and  pattern.  The  shaping 
influences  of  classicism  are  thus  everywhere  apparent, 
though  in  varying  degrees,  in  the  development  of  litera- 
ture on  the  technical  side. 

This  is  evident  if  we  consider  the  general  history  of 
style.  The  absurd  attempts  of  such  men  as  Gabriel 
Harvey,  WebSe,  and  Stanyhurst  to  reconstruct  English 
prosody  upon  the  strict  model  of  Greek  and  Latin 
quantitative  metre — attempts  which  for  a  time  almost 
seduced  Spenser — now  excite  amusement  only;  but  put 
into  their  proper  perspective  they  prove  at  least  that 
classical  studies  had  awakened  English  writers  to  the 
need  of  reform  in  the  manner  and  diction  of  their  poetry. 
That  reform  Wyatt  and  Surrey  did  much  to  carry  out, 
with  the  help  of  Italian  and  French  teachers  who  had 
themselves  sat  at  the  feet  of  the  ancients.  The  begin- 
nings of  modern  English  poetry  are  thus  closely  con- 
nected with  humanism.  All  over  Europe  about  the  same 
time  we  see  vernacular^prose^struggling  to  lift  itself  to 
the  high  level  oTthe  classical  tongues.  Men  who  had 
spent  their  days  and  nights  over  the  pages  of  the  Latin 
masters,  and  had  felt  the  lucidity  and  elegance  of  what 
they  read,  made  laborious  efforts  to  impart  something 
of  the  same  distinctive  qualities  to  the  rude  and  shape- 
less materials  with  which  they  had  to  work.  Careful 
study  was  now  accordingly  given  to  rhetorical  tech- 
nique ;  writers  came  to  seek  style  and  to  pursue  it  apart 
from  any  question  of  its  value  in  respect  of  the  things 
they  wanted  to  say.  An  intemperate  enthusiasm  for 
mere  form,  an  inordinate  devotion  to  figure  and  phrase, 


Evolution  of  Literary  Forms  237 

and  many  extraordinary  experiments  in  the  improve- 
ment of  native  speech  were  the  result.  We  see  this  in 
the  strained  and  overloaded  language  of  the  French 
rhetoriqueurs,  in  the  reform  programme  of  the  Pleiade, 
and  in  Du  Bellay's  contention  that  the  French  tongue 
could  acquire  real  excellence  and  polish  only  by  deliber- 
ate imitation  of  Greek  and  Latin  models.  We  see  it  in 
the  alto  estilo  of  the  Spanish  Guevara,  which  came  into 
England  through  the  translations  of  Berners  and  North, 
We  see  it  in  the  florid  mannerisms  of  Sidney's 
"Arcadia " ;  in  the  elaborate  artifices  of  Lyly's 
"  Euphues  " ;  in  the  fashionable  Italianism  of  the  cour- 
tiers, the  pedantry  of  the  schoolmaster,  and  the  grand- 
iloquence of  the  fantastical  Spaniard  in  "Love's 
Labour's  Lost." 

The  development  of  the  more  important  forms  of 
literature  during  the  period  of  the  Renaissance  was, 
like  the  evolution  of  the  sense  of  style  in  general,  largely 
the  consequence  of  classical  inspiration  and  example.  A 
considerable  portion  of  the  history  of  Renaissance 
literature  is,  in  fact,  concerned  with  the  revival  and 
adaptation  of  ancient  types. 

Thus,  for  instance,  while  ample  room  had  been  found 
in  mediaeval  literature  for  ridicule,  scorn,  invective,  and 
reproof,  the  satiric  spirit  had  expressed  itself  in  such 
forms  as  were  at  hand  and  already  popular  for  other  pur- 
poses; the  form  of  allegory,  for  instance,  in  Langland's 
"  Piers  Plowman  " ;  the  form  of  beast-story  in  "  Reinecke 
Fuchs."  Such  miscellaneous  vehicles  of  satire,  as  I 
need  scarcely  say,  have  continued  to  be  used  in  all 
literatures  down  to  our  own  day.  But  at  the  time  of  the 
Renaissance  the  satire  evolved  as  a  regular  specific 
type;  and  the  sources  of  this  formal  satire,  as  written 
by  such  men  as  Ariosto  in  Italy,  Regnier  in  France, 
Gascoigne,   Hall,   and  Donne  in   England,   are  to  be 


238  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

sought  in  the  works  of  the  Roman  satirists,  Horace, 
Juvenal,  and  Persius. 

The  history  of  another  humanistic  revival  which  for 
a  time  enjoyed  enormous  vogue — the  pastoral — is  in  the 
range  of  its  developments  and  influence  at  once  more 
varied  and  more  interesting.  The  pastoral  poem  origin- 
ated in  the  actual  folk-songs  of  the  Dorian  Greeks,  and 
was  thus  at  the  outset,  like  our  own  ballads,  entirely 
popular  in  character.  Its  formal  beginnings  date  from 
the  "Idylls"  of  the  Syracusan  poet  Theocritus,  about 
270  B.C.  These  "Idylls,"  or  "little  pictures,"  are  not 
sham  pastorals;  there  is  true  feeling  for  nature  in  them, 
and  plenty  of  fidelity  to  the  facts  they  purport  to  de- 
scribe. But,  products  of  the  sophisticated  Alexandrian 
age,  they  are  by  no  means  free  from  the  prevailing  taint 
of  artificiality;  and  in  the  work  of  those  who  followed 
and  imitated  Theocritus  this  taint  spread,  till  artifice 
finally  destroyed  reality.  Thus  the  pastoral  became  a 
pure  literary  convention,  cultivated  by  schplars  who 
were  far  more  concerned  about  the  form  than  about  the 
substance  of  their  writing;  it  no  longer  dealt  with  the 
life  of  actual  shepherds;  everything  was  idealised  in 
it  out  of  all  semblance  to  its  supposed  original ;  homely 
detail  vanished  before  idyllic  sentimentalism  and  the 
refinements  of  academic  taste;  natural  scenery  and  rural 
manners  were  displaced  by  the  landscape  of  Arcadia  and 
the  simplicity  of  the  Golden  Age.  Such  was  the  con- 
ventional form  which  pastoral  poetry  assumed  in  the 
"Bucolica"  of  Vergil.  Such  was  the  conventional  form 
which  it  assumed  under  the  influences  of  classical  enthu- 
siasm among  the  humanists  of  the  Renaissance.  In  this 
revival,  though  Theocritus  was  not  neglected,  Vergil 
was  the  principal  model ;  and  thus  the  modern  pastoral 
in  its  origin  was  but  the  imitation  of  an  imitation,  at 
two  removes  from  reality.     At  first  even  the  Latin  hexa- 


Pastoral  Literature  239 

meter  was  adopted,  as  by  both  Dante  and  Petrarch,  and 
this  tradition  was  carried  on  by  later  Italian  humanists, 
who,  as  we  shall  presently  see,  were  so  devoted  to  the 
ancient  tongues  as  to  despise  vernacular  verse.  Among 
these  there  is  one  who  on  account  of  the  extraordinary 
European  vogue  of  his  "Eclogues"  deserves  special 
mention — Giovanni  Battista  Spagnuoli,  better  known 
under  his  pen-name  of  Mantuanus.  Shakespeare's  re- 
ference to  him  as  the  "good  old  Mantuan  "  in  "Love's 
.  Labour's  Lost "  (iv.  2)  gives  the  English  reader  a 
nodding  acquaintance  with  him,  and  also  serves  to  in- 
dicate the  extent  of  his  reputation.  In  England,  as  on 
the  Continent,  his  poems  were  held  in  the  highest  esteem 
among  the  learned;  in  this  country,  according  to  six- 
teenth century  evidence,  they  were  even  preferred  to 
Vergil's  "Bucolica,"  *  while  their  use  as  a  regular  Latin 
textbook  in  English  schools  explains  Shakespeare's 
familiarity  with  them.  Great  as  was  the  impulse  which 
they  gave  to  the  same  form  of  literature  in  France  and 
England,  however,  pastoral  poetry  in  these  countries, 
as  with  the  later  idyllists  in  Italy,  practically  abandoned 
Latin  in  favour  of  vernacular  verse.  Pastoral  poetry 
thus  begins  a  new  chapter  of  its  history  as  a  recognised 
form  of  modern  literature.  The  "Eclogues "  of  Clement 
Maroi  (1496-1544)  mark  the  opening  of  this  chapter 
in  France.  In  England,  while  the  "  Eclogues "  of 
Alexander  Barclay  (1475-1552)  and  of  Barnabe  Googe 
(1540-94)  call  for  passing  reference  on  the  ground  of 
their  priority,  the  real  foundations  of  the  regular 
pastoral  were  laid  in  Spenser's  "Shepheardes  Calender  " 
(1579).  In  this  poem,  as  in  our  Renaissance  literature 
at  large,  we  see  how  the  influence  of  classicism  affected 
English  literature  in  two  ways — directly  and  indirectly; 

*  See   Farnaby's   preface   to   George   Turberville's   translation  of 
them,  1567. 


240  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

directly,  as  the  humahistic  spirit  was  revived  by  the 
first-hand  study  of  Greek  and  Latin  writers ;  indirectly, 
as  it  was  transmitted  by  the  literatures  of  Italy  and 
France.  It  is  therefore  a  point  of  importance  that 
Spenser's  acknowledged  masters  in  the  bucolic  art  are 
not  Theocritus  and  Vergil  only,  but  also  Mantuanus, 
Petrarch,  Boccaccio,  Sannazaro,  and  Marot.  The 
attempt  which  Spenser  makes  to  break  away  in  one 
respect  from  the  strict  conventions  of  the  pastoral  should 
also  be  noted.  That  convention  demanded  absolute 
refinement  of  diction  and  the  repudiation  of  everything 
suggestive  of  the  rough  realities  of  rustic  speech. 
Spenser  in  places  employs  a  strange  and  rather  uncouth 
kind  of  language — in  part  archaic,  in  part  dialectic — 
which  is  obviously  meant  to  bring  Arcadia  nearer  to  our 
English  fields,  and  to  touch  artifice  with  something  of 
the  freshness  of  truth.  Most  readers  to-day  feel,  in- 
deed, that  such  combination  of  stereotyped  machinery 
with  this  amount  of  rustic  realism  is  a  mistake;  that, 
in  fact,  it  serves  only  to  make  the  absurdities  of  the 
machinery  itself,  by  contrast,  the  more  glaringly 
apparent.  But  our  present  judgment  in  the  matter 
goes,  for  the  moment,  for  nothing.  That  Spenser 
should  even  to  this  extent  have  asserted  his  independ- 
ence of  academic  rule,  and,  while  accepting  in  general 
the  formalism  of  pastoral  poetry,  should  thereby  have 
sought  to  effect  at  least  a  slight  return  to  nature — that 
is  one  point  for  emphasis.  Another  is  the  opposition 
of  the  classic  theorist  to  the  innovation.  That  opposi- 
tion was  not  based  upon  the  broad  aesthetic  considera- 
tions to  which  I  have  just  referred.  It  was  inspired 
wholly  by  slavish  reverence  for  antiquity,  to  which,  as 
the  educational  movements  of  the  Renaissance  have 
already  shown  us,  classicism  too  often  led.  Sir  Philip 
Sidney,  to  whom  the  "Calender"  was  dedicated,  found 


Pastoral  Literature  241 

fault  with  this  experiment  in  style  because  "neyther 
Theocritus  in  Greeke,  Virgill  in  Latin,  nor  Sanazar  in 
Italian  did  affect  it."  Meanwhile  the  pastoral  spirit  was 
for  a  time  so  strong  that  it  not  only  expressed  itself 
directly  in  the  specific  bucolic  poem,  but  overflowed  into 
other  forms  of  literature  as  well.  It  overflowed  into  the 
drama,  as  in  Politian's  "Orfeo"  (1492),  Tasso's 
"Aminta"  (1573),  and  Guarini's  "Pastor  Fido"  (1590); 
in  Peele's  "Arraignment  of  Paris "  (circa  1581) ;  in 
Fletcher's  "Faithful  Shepherdess"  (1608),  and  Jonson's 
"Sad  Shepherd"  (1637).  It  thus  became  one  of  the 
elements  which  combined  in  the  production  of  that 
special  form  of  dramatic  entertainment  which  Italian 
influences  made  popular  in  England — the  Mask.  Pas- 
toralism  was  also  a  chief  impulse  behind  the  prose 
fiction  of  the  Renaissance.  It  inspired  Sannazaro's 
"Arcadia"  (1534),  the  earliest  work  of  its  school,  and, 
judged  by  its  generative  power,  one  of  the  most  im- 
portant. It  gave  birth  to  an  enormous  romantic  litera- 
ture in  Spain.  It  blended  with  other  elements  in  the 
production  of  Sidney's  "Arcadia,"  Lodge's  "Rosa- 
lynde,"  and  Greene's  "Pandosto,"  and,  much  later,  of 
the  Heroic  Romance  in  France.  In  strict  conformity 
with  classic  tradition,  pastoral  machinery  was  also  used 
for  the  purpose  of  elegy,  as  in  Spenser's  "Astrophel" 
and  Milton's  "Lycidas."  This  brief  sketch  does  not 
exhaust  the  interest  of  pastoralism  in  Renaissance  litera- 
ture. But  it  will  suffice  to  indicate  something  of  its 
range  and  bearings. 

Turning  next  to  the  whole  body  of  narrative  litera- 
ture in  prose  and  verse,  we  find  that  here  the  direct 
effect  of  classicism  on  method  and  structure  was  rela- 
tively inconsiderable.  In  prose,  the  short  tale  of  the 
Italian  story-tellers  and  their  French  and  English 
followers,  the  fully  developed  romance,  like  the 
Q 


242  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

"Arcadia"  just  mentioned,  and  the  entire  breed  of 
Lyly's  "Euphues,"  and  such  picaresque  novels  as 
Nash's  "Unfortunate  Traveller,"  were  independent 
excursions  into  a  field  in  which  it  would  have  been 
useless  to  look  back  to  Greece  or  Rome  for  practical 
help.  In  poetic  narrative,  where  plenty  of  such  help 
might  be  had  for  the  asking,  Renaissance  literature  still 
developed  freely,  and  along  the  general  lines  of 
mediaeval  verse  romance  rather  than  those  of  the  Greek 
and  Latin  epic.  Numerous  as  are  their  classical  bor- 
rowings in  detail,  there  is  little  trace  of  formal  Homeric 
or  Vergilian  influences  in  such  rambling  and  discursive 
chivalrous  romances  as  Boiardo's  "  Orlando  Inna- 
morato,"  for  example,  or  Ariosto's  more  famous  sequel 
to  this,  "Orlando  Furioso."  These  things,  like  the 
great  English  poem,  which  was  largely  inspired  by  and 
fashioned  upon  them,  Spenser's  "Faery  Queene,"  have 
a  very  complex  ancestry;  but,  so  far  as  fundamentals 
of  structure  are  concerned,  classic  example  did  little  to 
make  them  what  they  are.  Much  critical  talk  upon 
the  subject,  however,  naturally  turned  attention  to  the 
exact  form  of  the  classic  epic,  and  here  and  there  efforts 
were  made  to  reproduce  this  with  some  degree  of  pre- 
cision. In  general,  as  we  might  expect,  the  result  was 
a  failure,  as  in  the  Latin  epics  on  Christian  subjects 
which  appeared  in  Italy,  and  which  were  scarcely  more 
than  academic  exercises,  and  in  Ronsard's  unfinished 
"La  Franciade."  But  occasionally  a  true  poet  arose 
who  contrived  to  put  the  life  of  his  own  genius  into 
long-dead  forms.  This  was  done  particularly  by 
Camoens  in  his  "Os  Lusiadas"  (1572),  and  by  Tasso 
in  his  "Gerusalemme  Liberata"  (1575) — the  two  Euro- 
pean poems  which  together  represent  the  highest 
achievement  in  the  true  epic  revival  till  we  come  to 
"Paradise    Lost."      Both    are    constructed    upon    the 


The  Renaissance  Drama  243 

regular  epic  formula,  with  the  required  "supernatural 
machinery,"  battles,  councils,  and  episodes.  But  in 
both  the  plot  is  diversified  by  a  plentiful  admixture  of 
materials  which,  strictly  speaking,  scarcely  harmonise 
with  the  design.  This  is  especially  the  case  with  the 
"Gerusalemme,"  and  one  of  the  most  suggestive  points 
about  it  is  that,  just  as  Vergil  was  at  his  best  when  he 
was  not  imitating  Homer,  so  Tasso  was  at  his  best  when 
he  was  not  imitating  Vergil.  The  "epic"  part  of  his 
poem  is  correct  and  dull.  It  is  in  virtue  of  its  inter- 
woven stories  of  a  quite  romantic  and  sentimental  cast 
that  it  keeps  its  place  among  literature's  abiding  things. 
Finally,  we  have  to  touch  upon  what  is  really  the 
most  important  division  of  our  present  inquiry — thgjjiirt 
played  by  classicism  in  the  evolution  of  the  Renais- 
s»ncej^ama7"T  have  already  pointed  out  the  effbr  of 
supposing  that  this  drama  was  entirely  a  product  of 
the  revival  of  learning.  But  it  was  very  largely  in  the 
school  of  the  ancient  playwrights,  after  all,  that  the  men 
of  the  sixteenth  century  learned  their  structural  prin- 
ciples, and  the  influences  of  humanism  were  therefore 
of  immense  importance  in  the  transition  from  rude 
experiments  to  the  forms  of  art.  The  comedies  of 
Plautus  and  Terence,  and  the  tragedies  of  Seneca — 
subjects  of  ardent  study  to  begin  with  by  those  whose 
interest  in  literature  was  entirely  academic — thus  pre- 
sently became  things  of  practical  power  in  the  develop- 
ment of  the  public  stage,  first  in  Italy,  and  then,  in  part 
immediately  and  in  part  through  Italian  imitations,  in 
France  and  England.  But  while  the  classical  movement 
must  generally  be  accounted  one  of  the  principal  factors 
in  the  history  of  the  Renaissance  drama,  it  did  not  by 
any  means  operate  everywhere  with  equal  force.  It  was 
far  less  effective,  for  instance,  in  comedy  than  in 
tragedy;  for  in  comedy  the  popular  spirit  was  strong, 


244  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

while  tragedy  in  its  re-birth  was  almost  wholly  scholarly 
and  imitative.  Thus,  to  refer  only  to  our  English  drama, 
while  our  first  regular  comedy,  "Roister  Doister,"  was 
written  by  an  Eton  schoolmaster  for  performance  by 
his  boys  in  place  of  the  Latin  plays  then  common'  in 
all  the  centres  of  learning,  and  while  the  lines  of  the 
plot  avowedly  follow  those  of  Plautus  and  Terence,  the 
substance  and  style  are  of  native  origin,  and  much  of 
the  characterisation  combines  features  of  the  old  morali- 
ties with  those  of  the  Latin  comic  stage.  Tragedy,  on 
the  other  hand,  began  with  a  resolute  effort  to  discard 
the  popular  traditions  of  the  earlier  drama  entirely,  and 
to  reproduce  with  servile  accuracy  the  classical  system 
as  represented  in  its  severest  form  by  the  plays  of  the 
Latin  Seneca.  But  here,  more  clearly  than  in  any  other 
aspect  of  Renaissance  literature,  we  note  the  varying 
strength  of  the  classic  revival.  Italy  and  France 
accepted  Senecan  tragedy,  with  all  its  apparatus  and 
rules,  its  chorus,  its  stately  declamation,  its  total  want 
of  action,  its  absolute  rejection  of  colloquialism,  realism 
and  humorous  relief,  its  unities  of  time,  place  and 
action.  This  was  the  form  adopted  in  Tassoni's 
"Sofonisba,"  the  first  "regular"  tragedy  of  modern 
literature,  and  in  Jodelle's  "Cleop&tre,"  the  influence  of 
which  was  soon  reinforced  by  the  more  successful  work 
of  Gamier  and  Hardy.  Our  own  "Gorboduc"  was 
similarly  an  attempt  to  put  Senecan  tragedy  into  Eng- 
lish dress,  and  for  some  years  after  its  production 
English  scholars  did  their  utmost  to  naturalise  this  type 
of  play.  But,  despite  their  prestige  and  power,  the 
popular  traditions  of  the  English  stage  and  the  peculiar 
qualities  of  English  taste  were  too  much  for  them,  and 
they  failed.  From  this  time  onward,  the  drama  in 
England  and  the  drama  in  France  (that  of  Italy  soon 
becomes  immaterial)  pursued  entirely  different  courses. 


English  Blank  Verse  245 

Though  French  tragedy  modified  the  strict  Senecan 
convention  in  many  particulars,  it  remained  essentially 
"classic"  in  tone  and  character.  In  England  the 
"romantic"  drama,  as  it  was  established  by  the  "uni- 
versity wits "  and  as  it  culminated  in  Shakespeare, 
repudiated  academic  authority  and  developed  freely  in 
accordance  with  its  own  spirit  and  needs. 

One  most  important  contribution  which  classicism 
made  to  the  form  of  English  poetry  is  none  the  less 
suggested  at  this  point,  though  even  here  Italy  was  the 
intermediary — I  mean  blank  verse.  Italian  poets  in 
their  attempts  to  adapt  classical  metres  to  the  conditions 
of  their  own  tongue  had  struck  out,  among  other  ex- 
periments, the  unrhymed  hendecasyllabic  measure 
which  they  called  versi  sciolti,  or  "untied  lines."  This 
measure  the  Earl  of  Surrey  transplanted  to  English 
poetry  in  his  version  (circa  1535)  of  two  books  of  the 
"Aeneid,"  the  mere  dropping  of  the  light  terminal 
syllable  which  the  constitution  of  the  language  made 
inevitable  in  Italian  turning  the  hendecasyllabic  into  our 
own  decasyllabic  line.  Very  little  use  was  made  of 
this  importation  in  non-dramatic  poetry  till  Milton  took 
it  up  and  showed  its  full  possibilities  in  "Paradise  Lost." 
But  with  "Gorboduc  "  it  passed  at  once  into  the  drama, 
its  introduction  there  being  merely  a  part  of  the  general 
classicism  of  the  play.  Fortunately  it  was  not  allowed 
to  disappear  with  the  rest  of  the  machinery  of  Senecan 
tragedy,  nor  was  it  relegated  to  the  dust-heap  with  other 
attempts  to  naturalise  foreign  forms  in  English  verse. 
Marlowe  in  his  "Tamburlaine  the  Great"  in  turn  gave 
it  to  the  romantic  stage,  where  its  success  was  immedi- 
ately assured.  We  now  think  of  it,  indeed,  as  a  char- 
acteristic feature  of  our  romantic  drama.  But  we  must 
not  forget  that  it  was,  in  fact,  a  product  of  classicism 
as  interpreted  by  Italy. 


246  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

In  closing  this  part  of  our  inquiry,  I  may  fittingly 
lay  stress  upon  two  important  points. 

In  the  first  place,  it  has  become  evident  that,  though 
humanism  did  much  directly  and  indirectly  to  change 
the  spirit  and  the  currents  of  literature,  its  main  in- 
fluences are  to-be-  s&ught4a^he-4ev£lopmeatjaLlech- 
juqile^  Alike  by  precept  and  example  classicism  taught 
the  value  of  form  and  beauty,  and  was  thus  pre- 
eminently effective  in  the  evolution  of  literature  as  an 
art.  It  is  at  this  point  particularly,  therefore,  that  we 
connect  the  beginnings  of  modern  literature  with  the 
revival  of  learning. 

At  the  same  time  proof  has  also  been  afforded  of  the 
statement  made  at  the  opening  of  this  chapter  that  in 
European  Renaissance  literature,  considered  in  the 
mass,  differences  coexist  with  general  similarities.  The 
history  of  the  classical  movement  and  its  effects  is  very 
interesting  in  this  context.  In  England  the  "Latinisa- 
tion  of  culture  "  (to  use  Burckhardt's  phrase)  was  never 
nearly  so  thorough  and  complete  as  in  Italy  and  France. 
The  conjunction  of  these  two  names  must  not  indeed 
mislead  us.  Classicism  meant  one  thing  for  Italian 
literature,  and  another  and  quite  different  thing  for 
French  literature.  In  Italy  it  waj3__Qn  .the-  whole  dis- 
tinctly unfa3murahle.-to  vernacular  progress.  The  lead- 
ing humanists  despised  their  native  tongue,  sneered  at 
the  "Divine  Comedy"  as  a  poem  "for  bakers  and 
cobblers,"  and  from  the  time  of  the  "Decamerone  "  till 
that  of  the  Italian  lyrics  of  Lorenzo  de'  Medici  and 
Politian  gave  themselves  up  to  the  task  of  reviving 
Cicero  and  Vergil.  Then,  even  when  a  vernacular 
movement  began,  when  literature  became  popular  in- 
stead of  merely  scholarly,  and  when  the  Florentine 
Academy  solemnly  decided  that  a  man  of  wit  and  learn- 
ing might  write  in  Italian  without  losing  caste,  classical 


Classicism  and  English  Literature      247 

influences  were  still  so  overpoweringly  strong  that 
Italian  genius  turned  all  too  soon  to  the  cultivation  of 
form  and  style,  and  an  early  decline  was  the  inevitable 
consequence.  In  the  great  literature  of  the  French 
Renaissance,  from  Marot  and  Rabelais  at  one  end  to 
Montaigne  and  Regnier  at  the  other,  the  influence 
of  humanism  was  very  different*  Instead  of  checking 
vernacular  effort,  the  classic  revival  nourished  the 
national  genius,  and  stirred  individual  energy;  instead  of 
breeding  the  spirit  of  slavish  pedantry,  it  gave  quicken- 
ing impulse  to  originality.  Yet  while  the  distinction 
between  Italy  and  France  must  be  carefully  marked,  the 
contrast  between  both  of  them  and  England  is  far  more 
apparent.  For  obvious  reasons,  even  France  was  much 
nearer  than  England  to  the  sources  of  Latin  culture. 
Our  English  temperament,  our  social  conditions,  the 
force  of  the  national  spirit,  and  the  ingrained  romantic 
bias  of  our  race  combined  to  resist  the  imposition  of 
alien  tastes.  Thus,  though  humanism  penetrated  and 
pervaded  English  Renaissance  literature  through  and 
through,  classicism,  despite  persistent  efforts,  failed  to 
obtain  controlling  power  over  it.  In  Italy  it  hampered 
the  free  growth  of  national  genius.  In  France  the 
period  of  the  later  Renaissance  witnessed  the  formation 
of  that  classical  ideal  which  was  to  dominate  all  French 
literature  till  the  thirties  of  the  nineteenth  century. 
Classicism,  one  only  of  the  many  streams  by  which 
English  literature  was  fed,  did  not  become  a  main 
stream  till  the  so-called  "  Augustan  "  era. 

That  the  classicism  of  the  Renaissance  was,  as  I 
have  already  said,  fraught  for  literature  with  evil  as 
well  as  with  good  is  now  obvious.  When  humanism 
co-operated  with  individual  genius,  and  ancient  master- 
pieces were  regarded  only  as  an  inspiration  and  a  guide, 
then  the  results  were  wholly  excellent;  it  is  indeed  to 


248  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

this  co-operation  that  we  owe  much  that  is  greatest  in 
Renaissance  literature.  When  healthy  admiration  de- 
generated into  superstitious^ veneration,  and  lifeless 
copying  took  the  place  of  personal  effort,  then  human- 
istic studies  only  led  the  emancipated  intellect  once  more 
back  into  bondage  to  the  past.  Unfortunately  the 
mania  for  everything  antique  which  at  first  led  scholars 
all  over  Europe  to  Latinise  their  own  names  and  give 
Latin  names  to  their  children,  and  later  wrought  such 
havoc  with  education,  also  brought  about  the  spread  of 
the  narrowest  pedantry  in  literature.  The  enthusiasm 
for  the  classics  which  was  a  principal  cause  of  the 
splendid  literary  outburst  of  the  Renaissance  was  thus 
also  in  large  measure  responsible  for  the  stagnation 
which  followed  with  the  growth  of  pseudo-classicism. 


V. — THE  RELIGIOUS   RENAISSANCE  AND  LITERATURE 

It  remains  for  us  to  consider  in  what  ways  and 
to  what  extent  European  Renaissance  literature  was 
affected  by  the  religious  revival  and  the  intellectual 
and  moral  stir  by  which  this  was  accompanied. 

Manifestly,  as  classicism  in  the  main  directly  influ- 
enced literature  as  an  art,  so  the  religious  revival,  on  the 
other  hand,  left  its  impress  chiefly  on  its  spirit,  tone, 
and  aims.  Yet  in  one  respect  it  counted  also  as  a 
factor  in  the  development  of  literary  form.  It  helped 
the  establishment  of  a  good  practical  prose  style. 

This  was  the  natural  result  of  the  democratic 
tendency  of  Protestantism,  its  propagandist  energies, 
and — the  product  of  these  in  combination — its  earnest 
desire  to  reach  beyond  the  circle  of  scholarship  to  the 
common  man.  In  Germany,  though  the  Reformation 
was  otherwise  almost  fatal  to  literature,  Luther's  tX^^Sz 
lation^of  the  Bible  and  his  miscellaneous  religious  and 


The  Reformation  and  Literature       249 

social  writings  fixed  the  standard  of  the  language  for 
general  use.     In  France  the  popular  spirit  of  Protes- 
tantism was  of  great  service  in  breaking  down  the  affec- 
tations  of    literary   and   aristocratic   cliques   and   sub- 
stituting a  current  style  having  the  virtues  at  least  of 
plainness,  directness,  and  simplicity.     The  work  of  the 
Protestant  ^larot,  the  bent  of  whose  genius  was  strongly 
against  classicism,  and  who  in  particular  rejected  the 
pedantry    of    the    rhetoriqueur   school,    represents    the 
effects  of  this  popular  spirit  in  verse.     In  prose  it  is 
recognised  that  Calvin's  "Institutes"  (in  the  translation 
made  almost  immediately  by  the  author  himself  from 
the  Latin  in  which  they  were  first  written)  were  of  great 
importance  to  French  style  at  a  time  when  it  was  throw- 
ing off   its   mediaeval   heritage   and  evolving   into   its 
modern  form.     But  most  of  all  in  England  are  we  able 
to  observe  what  religious  activity  did  for  the  vernacular. 
Even  Wyclif,  it  will  be  remembered,  had  not  only  pro- 
duced an  English  translation  of  the  Bible,  but  had  also 
written  tractates  in  plain  English  that  he  might  appeal 
broadly  to  the  "vulgar."     In  the  fifteenth  century  the 
independent  theologian,  Reginald  Pecock,  while  falling 
foul  of  both  Lollards  and  Churchmen,  did  yeoman  ser- 
vice by  enlarging  the  powers  of   English  for  higher 
exposition  and  argument.     Tyndale's  New  Testament, 
directly  prompted  by  and  in  part  based  upon  Luther's 
German  and  Erasmus's  Latin  versions,   also  deserves 
mention    among   tne   works   which   helped   to   fashion 
English  at  a  critical  juncture  in   its  history,   and  its 
interest  is  the  greater  because  it  was  afterwards  followed 
so  closely  by  the  scholars  responsible  for  the  Authorised 
Translation  of  the  scriptures.       More  than  any  other 
volume  ever  published,  this  "greatest  monument  by  far 
of  Jacobean  prose "  has  been  a  constant  force  in  the 
shaping  of  English  style,  and  the  special  significance  of 


250  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

it  from  our  present  point  of  view  lies  in  the  fact  that, 
like  the  Book  of  Common  Prayer,  which  also  exerted  a 
wide  though  indirect  influence,  it  was  destined  not  for 
any  one  class  of  the  community,  but  for  all  sorts  and 
conditions  of  men,  and  was  therefore  couched  in  a 
language  "understanded  of  the  people."  The  stress 
has  therefore  again  to  be  thrown  upon  the  democratic 
spirit  of  Protestantism,  and  upon  the  way  in  which 
this  aided  in  the  establishment  of  a  standard  prose 
which  should  be  in  the  best  sense  of  both  the  words  at 
once  scholarly  and  popular. 

It  is  only,  however,  when  we  turn  from  these  more 
superficial  things  to  the  consideration  of  the  moral 
meaning  of  literature  as  an  interpretation  of  life,  that 
we  are  in  a  position  to  realise  the  enormous  influence  of 
the  religious  revival. 

Negative  evidence  of  its  power  is  afforded  by  the 
literature  of  Italy.  I  have  already  spoken  of  the  paradox 
of  the  Italian  Renaissance,  in  which  devotion  to  learn- 
ing and  a  passionate  love  of  beauty  co-existed  with 
private  profligacy  and  political  corruption,  and  I  have 
pointed  out  that  the  worst  feature  of  the  case  was  not 
the  colossal  wickedness  of  a  few  monsters  of  iniquity 
here  and  there,  but  the  laxity  and  flippancy  which 
characterised  society  in  general .  What  Renaissance 
Italy  needed  was  a  spiritual  awakening  to  temper  its 
lust  for  life,  check  the  conversion  of  liberty  into  licence, 
and  give  direction  to  its  artistic  energies.  But  the 
mission  of  Savonarola  failed,  and  thenceforth  sensuality 
and  indifference  spread  unhindered.  The  result  is  seen 
in  the  literature  of  the  age.  The  one  prevailing  defect 
of  that  literature  is  its  want  of  lofty  aim  and  moral  fibre. 
It  has  rarely,  very  rarely,  that  note  of  "  high^serkms- 
-ness  "  which  Matthew  Arnold  rightly  believed  to  be  one 
of  the  essential  elements  of  all  enduring  greatness  in 


Italian  Renaissance  Literature  251 

literature.  Its  preoccupation  with  form,  upon  which  we 
have  recently  touched,  was  indeed  in  part  the  conse- 
quence of  its  want  of  "sound  subject-matter  "  and  the 
.'  ispiration  of  a  large  and  generous  purpose.  Even  the 
finest  scholarship  ran  to  waste  in  the  cultivation  of 
Ciceronian  purity  in  prose  and  Vergilian  perfection  in 
verse,  because  in  a  world  which  comes  with  an  ever- 
lasting challenge  and  stimulus  to  those  who  have  eyes 
to  see  and  souls  to  understand,  neither  the  writer  nor 
his  public  cared  for  anything  beyond  such  dilettante 
trifling.  Of  the  fearful  abominations  by  which  a  good 
deal  of  this  Italian  Renaissance  literature  was  defiled  I 
need  not  further  speak;  my  passing  reference  in  an 
earlier  chapter  to  the  effusions  of  such  men  as  Filelfo, 
Poggio,  Aretino,  and  Beccadilli  will  suffice.  It  is  more 
to  the  point  to  dwell  for  a  moment  upon  the  quality 
of  work  which  we  can  still  read  and  enjoy.  Even  in 
this — even  in  the  poetry  of  Ariosto,  the  greatest  figure 
in  the  Italian  literature  of  the  central  Renaissance — 
the  moral  deficiencies  are  very  apparent.  To  appreciate 
these  we  have  only  to  glance  at  the  most  characteristic 
product  of  the  Italian  genius  at  this  time— the  chivalrous 
romance  in  verse.  The  foundations  of  Pulci's  "Mor- 
gante  Maggiore,"  and  of  Boiardo's  "Orlando  Inna- 
morato,"  and  of  Ariosto's  "Orlando  Furioso"  are 
furnished  by  the  old  romances  of  chivalry,  and  especially 
by  one  of  the  two  great  legend-cycles  of  the  middle 
ages — that  which  had  grown  up  about  Charlemagne 
and  his  paladins.  But  of  the  idealism  of  chivalry 
very  little  remains.  Brilliant,  vivacious,  full  of  wit 
and  fancy,  overflowing  with  the  zest  of  adventure,  these 
poems  have  really  nothing  in  them  to  remind  us  of  the 
stock  from  which  they  sprang,  save  the  names  of  some 
of  their  characters  and  the  groundwork  of  their  plots. 
To  bring  out  their  essential  features  by  contrast  we  need 


252  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

not  have  recourse  to  that  golden  book  of  knight-errantry, 
Malory's  "Morte   Darthur."     We  shall   find  contrast 
enough  in  "Don  Quixote,"  which,  burlesque  though  it 
is,   contains  infinitely  more  of  the  genuine  temper  of 
chivalry  than  these  masterpieces  of  a  totally  different 
order  of  genius,  with  their  polished  irony,  their  well- 
bred  banter,  their  pervasive  gaiety,  and  their  absolute 
lack  of  earnestness  and  enthusiasm.     Ariosto,  the  most 
consummate  artist  in  this  particular  kind  of  narrative, 
and  one  of  the  most  admirable  story-tellers  in  verse 
the  world  has  ever  seen,  wrote  confessedly  to  amuse  a 
careless,  pleasure-loving,  cynical,  and  degenerate  age; 
he  succeeded  in  his  object;  and  his  work  inevitably  took 
the  tone  and  colour  of  his  audience.     The  irony  and 
banter  are  indeed  so  prominent  in  his  poem,  he  is  so 
entirely  irreverent  in   spirit  and  tendency,   that  some 
modern  Italian  critics  have  attributed  to  him  the  inten- 
tion of  parodying  the  old  romances  of  chivalry.     It  is 
safer  to  assume  that  his  purpose  was  simply  to  adapt 
excellent  legendary  material  to  the  mental  and  moral 
tastes  of  his  time.     But,  though  no  actual  parody  can 
be  detected  in  it,  such  work  as  his  made  the  transition 
easy  from   romantic   epic   to   genuine   epic  burlesque. 
Tassoni's    "La    Secchia    Rapita,"    or    "Rape   of    the 
Bucket,"  which  turns  the  machinery  and  sentiments  of 
the  true  epic  into  ridicule  by  employing  them  in  con- 
nection with  an  absurdly  trivial  theme,  is,  like  our  own 
"Rape  of  the  Lock,"  a  significant  commentary  upon 
the  character  of  the  age  which  produced  it;  though  we 
must  not  forget  that,  again  like  Pope's  mock-heroic, 
it  satirises  as  well  as  reflects.    A  generation  earlier  the 
great  epic  of  the  devout  Tasso  had  shown  the  religious 
stimulus  of  the  Counter-reformation.     Of  that  move- 
ment the  "Gerusalemme  Liberata,"  indeed,  is  the  one 
lasting  poetic  monument.     Its  central  theme — the  task 


Reformation  and  French  Literature    253 

of  the  first  crusade  under  Godfrey  of  Bouillon ;  its  pre- 
sentation of  the  character  of  the  leader,  in  whom  the 
qualities  of  the  pious  JEneas  are  curiously  blended  with 
those  of  the  new  missionary  heroes  of  the  Catholic  faith ; 
the  strenuous  religious  spirit  and  the  fervent  zeal  by 
which  it  is  pervaded :  all  these  things  combine  to  make 
it  a  thoroughly  typical  production.  But  it  is  not  only 
typical,  it  is  also  unique.  It  marks  both  the  beginning 
and  the  end  of  the  influence  of  the  Catholic  reaction  on 
literature. 

Turning  now  from  Italy  to  France,  we  perceive  at 
once  how  deeply  literature  gained  by  contact  with  the 
spirit  of  the  Reformation.  The  French  literature  of  the 
Renaissance  will  not,  indeed,  bear  comparison  with  the 
Italian  on  the  side  of  form  and  style ;  but  it  reveals,  on 
the  other  hand,  a  moral  vigour  and  a  vital  power  which 
amply  compensate  for  any  artistic  shortcomings,  and 
these  great  qualities,  while  traceable  in  part,  of  course, 
to  various  political  and  social  causes,  are,  as  Mr.  Tilley 
has  put  it,  due  "in  a  large  measure  to  the  fact  that 
in  Franpe^  the  "Renaissance  came  hand  in  haiid__with 
the  Reformation."*  At  the  outsetHPfbtestantism  was 
regarded  with  much  favour  by  many  of  the  great  French 
scholars,  like  Bud6,  and  intellectual  leaders,  like 
Rabelais,  who  were  quick  to  realise  its  intimate  con- 
nection with  the  general  movement  for  enlightenment 
and  freedom  of  thought.  It  was  not,  indeed,  till, 
with  Calvin,  dogmatism  and  bigotry  regained  their 
supremacy,  and  the  old  despotism  of  infallibility  was 
re-established  in  a  new  form,  that  humanism  definitely 
parted  company  with  the  reformed  theology.  The 
change  in  the  relations  of  the  two  revivals — the  intel- 
lectual and  the  religious — goes  far  to  account  for  the 

*  "  The  Literature  of  the  French  Renaissance :  Introductory 
Essay,"  p.  33- 


254  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

marked  difference  in  tone  between  the  French  literature 
of  the  earlier  and  that  of  the  later  Renaissance.  But 
meanwhile,  though  it  touched  only  the  few,  the  spirit 
of  the  Reformation  had  stirred  the  best  thought  of 
France  to  its  depths,  and  literature  was  filled,  in  con- 
sequence, with  the  strength  and  passion  of  life.  We 
feel  this,  for  example,  in  the  magnificent  earnestness, 
the  genuine  humanitarian  enthusiasm,  the  prophetic 
ardour  and  faith  which  are  everywhere  present  beneath 
the  wild  foolery  and  the  reckless  indecencies  of  Rabelais. 
Mr.  Tiliey  has  drawn  out  an  interesting  comparison 
between  this  great  representative  figure  of  the  French 
Renaissance  and  the  great  representative  figure  of  the 
same  age  in  Italy,  Ariosto.  "How  formless!  How 
crude  !  How  gross  !  How  full  of  cumbersome  details 
and  wearisome  repetitions  is  Rabelais !  How  limpid, 
how  harmonious  is  Ariosto !  What  perfection  of  style, 
what  delicacy  of  touch  !  He  never  wearies  us ;  he  never 
offends  our  taste.  And  yet  one  rises  from  the  reading 
of  Rabelais  with  a  feeling  of  buoyant  cheerfulness, 
while  Ariosto,  in  spite  of  his  wit  and  gaiety,  is  inex- 
pressibly depressing.  The  reason  is  that  the  one  bids 
us  hope,  the  other  bids  us  despair;  the  one  believes  in 
truth  and  goodness  and  in  the  future  of  the  human  race; 
the  other  believes  in  nothing  but  the  pleasures  of  the 
senses,  which  come  and  go  like  many-coloured  bubbles, 
and  leave  behind  them  a  boundless  ennui.  Rabelais 
and  Ariosto,"  the  writer  concludes — and  this  for  us  is 
the  capital  point  of  the  comparison — "are  true  types  of 
the  Renaissance  as  it  appeared  in  their  respective 
,  countries."*  Energising  qualities  of  faith  and  moral 
,-  purpose  which  are  wanting  in  the  Italian  give  essential 
greatness  to  the  Frenchman ;  and  the  difference  between 
the  two  is  in  part  the  difference  between  literature 
*  "  Introductory  Essay,"  pp.  38,  39. 


Reformation  and  English  Literature    255 

untouched  and  literature  touched  by  the  influences  of 
the  religious  revival. 
\  Yet,  as  no  reader  of  these  pages  will  need  to  be 
reminded,  it  is  in  the  English  literature  of  the  Renais- 
sance period  that  the  power  of  the  religious  revival  is 
Imost  fully  felt.  If  it  is  impossible  to  understand  this 
^literature  without  constant  reference  to  the  whole  com- 
plex of  results  which  followed  the  spread  of  humanism, 
it  is  equally  impossible  to  understand  it  without  constant 
reference  to  the  whole  complex  of  results  which  followed 
the  Reformation.  In  saying  this  I  am  not,  of  course, 
thinking  of  any  influence  directly  exerted  by  changes 
of  theological  doctrine  or  ecclesiastical  government.  I 
am  thinking  of  the  total  and  indirect  effect  wrought  by 
the  Reformation  in  the  political  and  moral  conditions 
and  temper  of  English  society.  To  the  Englishman  of 
that  age  the  Reformation  meant  much  more  than  a 
reconstruction  of  creed  and  church.  It  meant  the 
liberation  of  his  country  from  the  yoke  of  a  foreign 
potentate  in  the  person  of  the  Pope  of  Rome.  It  meant, 
after  a  sharp  collision  with  the  chief  champion  of  the 
papal  claims  and  the  Catholic  reaction,  Spain,  consoli- 
dation of  national  unity  and  a  tremendous  deepening 
of  the  national  self-consciousness.  It  meant,  in  conse- 
quence, the  enormous  growth  of  the  sense  of  England's 
greatness  and  destiny,  of  patriotism,  and  of  the  spirit 
of  enterprise  and  adventure.  The  minds  of  men  had 
been  stimulated  by  the  conflict  of  parties  and  by  all  the 
excitement  which  had  been  bred  by  the  religious 
upheaval,  while  the  comparative  peace  which  came  with 
the  settlement  under  Elizabeth  gave  them  the  oppor- 
tunity— and  this,  it  will  be  remembered,  at  a  period 
when  France  was  torn  by  fierce  religious  wars — of 
turning  their  attention  freely  to  the  newly  awakened 
intellectual    interests    of    their    time.      The    common 


256  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

conscience  had  also  been  stirred  by  the  open  Bible  of 
Protestantism  and  the  sermons  of  evangelical  preachers; 
the  atmosphere  had  been  charged  thereby  with  fresh 
emotional  elements,  and  a  current  of  strenuous  idealism 
had  been  set  flowing  far  and  wide  among  the  English 
people.  That  England,  as  Mr.  Symonds  has  said, 
"received  the  influences  of  both  Renaissance  and 
Reformation  simultaneously,"  is  thus  a  fact  of  the  most 
vital  importance  to  the  student  of  our  history.  It  was 
largely  owing  to  this  combination  of  forces  that  not 
only  intellectually,  but  also  morally  and  spiritually,  the 
Elizabethan  age  was  so  intensely  alive.  It  is  to  the 
same  combination  of  forces,  therefore,  that  we  may  in 
the  main  ascribe  that  intensity  of  life  which  was  the 
outstanding  feature  of  its  literature. 

We  must  be  careful  not  to  exaggerate  the  actual 
moral  power  of  the  Reformation  over  this  literature. 
While  the  spirit  of  humanism  and  the  spirit  of  the 
religious  revival  have  alike  to  be  reckoned  among  its 
formative  influences,  these  two  spirits  often  came  into 
conflict,  and  their  conflict  gave  rise  to  those  glaring 
contradictions  with  which  it  continually  surprises  us 
as  we  read.  No  one  who  knows  it  in  detail  will  deny 
that  there  is  much  in  it  to  justify  the  apprehensions  of 
those  who  watched  with  alarm  the  spread  of  Italian 
corruption  on  native  soil.  At  times,  as  in  "Hero  and 
Leander"  and  "Venus  and  Adonis"  (both  of  which, 
it  should  be  noted,  enjoyed  special  vogue  on  account 
of  their  open  licentiousness),  neo-paganism  runs  riot  in 
it.  At  times  its  passion  becomes  brutal  and  its  egotism 
defiant.  At  times  its  delight  in  life  and  in  the  things 
of  the  senses  degenerates  into  a  riot  of  imaginative 
extravagance.  We  have  only  to  turn  to  the  great  Eliza- 
bethan drama  to  perceive — and  we  can,  in  fact,  do  this 
without  travelling  beyond  the  pages  of  Shakespeare — 


Spenser  257 

how  often  the  taste  of  the  age  even  at  its  best  revelled 
in  motives  and  fancies  which  offend  us,  in  crude  violence 
and  in  gross  jesting,  from  which  we  now  recoil  shocked. 
Elsewhere,  as  in  the  typical  case  of  Marlowe,  we  have 
the  emancipated  genius  of  the  age  pursuing  an  erratic 
course,  unguided  and  unchecked  by  any  controlling 
sense  of  moral  law. 

All  qualifications  made,  however,  the  fact  remains 
that,  taken  in  the  mass,  English  Renaissance  literature 
is  a  wonderfully  healthy  minded  literature — a  literature 
which,  indeed,  is  often  extravagant,  fantastic,  and  even 
coarse,  but  which  is  at  the  same  time  fundamentally 
manly,  vigorous,  and  sound.  Very  little  of  the  radical 
depravity  or  the  peculiarly  debilitating  atmosphere  of 
Italian  paganism  is  anywhere  to  be  detected  in  it.  On 
the  contrary,  its  general  spirit  is  bracing;  it  shows  a 
steady  realisation  of  moral  values;  it  rings  true  to  the 
great  issues  of  life.  These  characteristics  are  explained 
by  the  application  of  Taine's  triple  formula  of  race, 
environment,  and  moment.  English  Renaissance 
literature  is  the  expression  of  the  genius,  energies,  and 
ideals  of  a  robust  race  in  a  mood  of  intellectual,  moral, 
and  spiritual  exaltation. 

At  this  point  stress  should  be  laid  upon  the  profound 
historical  interest  of  the  work  of  Spenser.  He  is  pre- 
eminently   tte^^regentatiy^ 

Renaissance,  his  relations  with  which  are  strikingly 
similar  to  those  of  Tennyson,  three  centuries  later,  with 
the  civilisation  of  the  Victorian  era.  All  the  great 
influences  of  the  age  met  and  blended  in  him,  and  that 
wonderful  creation,  "The  Faery  Queene,"  was  the  pro- 
duct of  their  harmonious  co-operation.  It  is  steeped  in 
humanism.  It  draws  much  of  its  inspiration  from  the 
classics  and  Italy.  The  love  of  sensuous  beauty  in 
nature,  in  art,  in  woman,  is  one  of  its  salient  charac- 

R 


258  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

teristics.  The  teachings  of  Greek  masters— of  Aristotle 
and  most  of  all  of  Plato,  whom  the  poet  had  studied 
mainly  through  Ficino's  commentary — are  conspicuous 
elements  in  its  texture  throughout.  It  joins  with  the 
scholar's  knowledge  of  and  devotion  to  pagan  literature 
the  Protestant's  familiarity  with  and  reverence  for  the 
Bible.  It  supports  the  cause  of  the  English  Church 
and  expounds  its  theology.  The  ardour  of  the  Reforma- 
tion sweeps  through  its  pages,  and  its  religious  spirit 
is  uncompromising  and  often  rancorous.  It  overflows 
with  patriotism,  with  love  of  England  and  hatred  of 
England's  enemies — Rome  and  Rome's  allies — and  with 
romantic  loyalty  to  England's  queen.  Its  moral  tone  is 
lofty  and  strenuous,  and  the  chivalrous  machinery  of 
Ariosto,  whom  Spenser  set  himself  to  "overgo,"  instead 
of  being  used  for  amusement  only,  is  loaded  with 
didactic  purpose,  and  made  the  allegorical  vehicle  of 
an  ethical  philosophy  which  itself^  injcoFpotates  the 
humanistic  with^the  Prgt^tant  ideal—  In  consonance, 
moreover,  with  the  positive  temper  which  the  Reforma- 
tion did  so  much  to  foster,  that  philosophy  is  directed  to 
large  practical  ends.  The  poet  does  not  seek  to  "justify 
the  ways  of  God  to  men."  In  the  true  spirit  of  an  age 
which  found  its  greatest  theological  thinker  in  Hooker 
and  its  greatest  master  of  method  in  Bacon,  he  makes 
it  his  object  "to  fashion  a  gentleman  or  noble  person 
in  vertuous  and  gentle  discipline."  It  is  not  the  least 
noteworthy  thing  about  his  palace  of  dainty  devices  that, 
built  though  it  seems  to  be  in  dreamland,  it  has  windows 
which  open  upon  the  broad  highways  of  the  world.* 
Spenser  holds  his  place  unchallenged,  therefore,  as 

*  The  practical  temper  of  the  Elizabethan  age,  its  "  profound 
interest  in  reality  as  opposed  to  abstractions,"  and  its  "  rich  feeling 
for  concrete  fact,"  have  been  well  brought  ont  by  Professor  Dowden. 
See  his  "  New  Studies  in  Literature,"  pp.  445-6.  Their  connection 
with  the  general  movement  of  the  Renaissance  will  now  be  clear.

gfrtrr*' 


Shakespeare  259 

the  poetical  interpreter  of  the  complex  forces  which 
shaped  the  life  of  Elizabethan  England.  Yet  it  was 
not,  after  all,  in  narrative  poetry,  nor  in  any  other 
purely  literary  form  of  art,  that  the  genius  of  Renais- 
sance England  found  its  fullest  expression.  It  was — 
and  the  fact  itself  is  significant — in  the  romantic  drama. 
Here  Shakespeare,  too,  becomes  representative.  We 
have  only  to  brush  away  all  the  accumulations  of 
critical  nonsense  about  his  "  universality "  and  his 
independence  of  time  and  place,  to  see  him  as  essen- 
tially a  man  of  his  age.  His  scholarship  was  meagre, 
and  as  his  was  evidently  one  of  those  completely  artistic 
natures  to  which  speculative  questions  and  spiritual 
interests  make  little  appeal,  religion  was  of  small 
account  in  moulding  his  genius.  But  his  curiously 
assimilative  mind  absorbed  all  the  influences  which  were 
at  work  about  him,  and  humanism  and  the  moral  vigour 
of  the  Reformation  alike  gave  form  and  pressure  to  his 
plays.  Narrow  in  patriotism,  yet  catholic  in  sympathy 
and  singularly  flexible  in  judgment,  intensely  alive  to 
the  pathos  and  beauty  and  no  less  to  the  sin  and  ugliness) 
which  make  up  the  mingled  yarn  of  human  life,  at 
once  idealistic  and  sensual,  romantic  in  temper,  yet,X 
full  of  the  sturdiest  common  sense,  he  does  indeed 
epitomise  many  of  the  qualities  which  gave  its  distinc- 
tive character  to  the  vehement,  virile,  many-sided  Eliza- 
bethan age.  About  him,  as  about  his  generation,  much 
of  the  old  order  still  clung;  he  was  often  cramped  by 
its  traditions,  hampered  by  its  prejudices,  and  entangled 
in  its  superstitions.  His  limitations,  which  are  fre- 
quently as  surprising  as  his  general  breadth  of  outlook, 
are  therefore  historically  even  more  instructive  than 
this.  But,  like  his  generation,  he  belongs  emphatically 
to  the  season  of  re-birth  and  the  great  period  of  tran- 
sition.    Nor,  as  I  need  hardly  add,  is  his  importance 


260  The  Story  of  the  Renaissance 

merely  national.  He  holds  the-paramouat -place  in  the 
litgraturej?!  fhp,  Rfjiajj^ajicfc^m -general.  His  position 
in  the  history  of  creative  achievement  is,  in  fact, 
analogous  to  that  of  his  foremost  contemporary  in  the 
history  of  thought.  In  Bacon,  as  we  have  seen,  the 
intellectual  movements  of  the  European  revival  cul- 
minated. In  Shakespeare  we  have  the  crowning 
incarnation  of  its  imaginative  genius.  The  one  stands 
for  the  scientific  interpretation  of  nature;  the  other  for 
the  dramatic  interpretation  of  life;  the  one  for  the 
literature  of  knowledge;  the  other  for  the  literature  of 
power.  Each  in  his  own  way  supreme,  they  together 
represent,  and  with  a  fullness  found  nowhere  else  amid 
all  the  varied  activities  of  contemporary  Europe,  the 
twofold  significance  of  the  Renaissance  as  the  .discovery 
at  once  of  the  world  and  of  man.
